CHAPTER 30 
Remedies against Pride 


SECTION I 
General Remedies 


We have already called the deadly or Capital sins the 
sources of all iniquity. They are the roots of the mighty tree 
of vice, and if we can destroy them the trunk and branches 
must soon decay. With them, therefore, we shall begin, 
following the example of Cassian and other spiritual writ- 
ers, who were so firmly convinced that if they could only 
rout these enemies the defeat of the others would be an easy 
task. 

St. Thomas gives us a profound reason for this. All sin, 
he says, proceeds from self-love, for we never commit sin 
without coveting some gratification for self. From self-love 
spring those three branches of sin mentioned by St. John: 
“the concupiscence of the flesh, the concupiscence of the 
eyes, and the pride of life” (J Jn. 2:16), which are love of 
pleasure, love of riches, and love of honors. Three of the 
deadly sins, lust, gluttony, and sloth, spring from love of 
pleasure, pride springs from love of honors, and covetous- 
ness from love of riches. The remaining two, anger and 
envy, serve all these unlawful loves. Anger is aroused by 
any obstacle which prevents us from attaining what we 
desire, and envy is excited when we behold anyone possess- 
ing what our self-love claims. These are the three roots of 
the seven deadly sins, and consequently of all the others. 


258 


Remedies against Pride 259 


| et these chiefs be destroyed and the whole army will soon 
ve routed. Hence we must vigorously attack these mighty 
>.ants who dispute our entrance to the promised land. 
The first and most formidable of these enemies is pride, 
hat inordinate desire of our own excellence, which 
:piritual writers universally regard as the father and king of 
ill the other vices. Hence Tobias, among the numerous 
zood counsels which he gave his son, particularly warns 
iim against pride: ‘“‘Never suffer pride to reign in thy mind 
or in thy words, for from it all perdition took its beginning.” 
(Job. 4:14). Whenever, therefore, you are attacked by this 
vice, which may justly be called a pestilence, defend your- 
self with the following considerations: 
_ First reflect on the terrible punishment which the angels 
Drought upon themselves by one sin of pride. They were in- 
peanitly cast from Heaven into the lowest depths of Hell. 
‘Consider how this fall transformed Lucifer, the prince of 
the angelic hosts, and the bright and beautiful star surpass- 
ing in splendor the sun itself. In one moment he lost all his 
glory, and became not only a demon but the chief of dem- 
ons. If pure spirits received such punishment, what can you 
expect, who are but dust and ashes? God is ever the same, 
and there is no distinction of persons before His justice. 
Pride is as odious to Him in a man as in an angel, while 
humility is equally pleasing to Him in both. Hence St. 
Augustine says, “Humility makes men angels, and pride 
makes angels devils.” And St. Bernard tells us, “Pride pre- 
cipitates man from the highest elevation to the lowest abyss, 
but humility raises him from the lowest abyss to the highest 
elevation. Through pride the angels fell from Heaven to 
Hell, and through humility man is raised from earth to 
Heaven.” 

After this, reflect on that astonishing example of humility 
given us by the Son of God, who for love of us took upon 
Himself a nature so infinitely beneath His own, and 


260 The Sinner’s Guide 


“became obedient unto death, even the death of the cross.” 
(Phil. 2:8). Let the example of your God teach you, O man, 
to be obedient. Learn, O dust, to humble yourself. Learn, O 
clay, to appreciate your baseness. Learn from your God, O 
Christian, to be ‘“‘meek and humble of heart.” (Matt. 11:29). 
If you disdain to walk in the footsteps of men, will you 
refuse to follow your God, who died not only to redeem us 
but to teach us humility? Look upon yourself and you will 
find sufficient motives for humility. Consider what you 
were before your birth, what you are since your birth, and 
what you will be after death. Before your birth you were, 
for a time, an unformed mass; now a fair but false exterior 
covers what is doomed to corruption; and in a little while 
you will be the food of worms. Upon what do you pride 
yourself, O man, whose birth is ignominy, whose life is mis- 
ery, whose end is corruption? If you are proud of your 
riches and worldly position, remember that a few years 
more and death will make us all equal. We are all equal at 
birth with regard to our natural condition; and as to the nec- 
essity of dying, we shall all be equal at death, with this im- 
portant exception: that those who possessed most during 
life will have most to account for in the day of reckoning. 

“Examine,” says St. Chrysostom, “the graves of the rich 
and powerful of this world, and find, if you can, some trace 
of the luxury in which they lived, of the pleasures they so 
eagerly sought and so abundantly enjoyed. What remains of 
their magnificent retinues and costly adornments? What re- 
mains of those ingenious devices destined to gratify their 
senses and banish the weariness of life? What has become 
of that brillant society by which they were surrounded? 
Where are the numerous attendants who awaited their com- 
mands? Nothing remains of their sumptuous banquets. The 
sounds of laughter and mirth are no longer heard; a somber 
silence reigns in these homes of the dead. But draw nearer 
and see what remains of their earthly tenements, their 


Remedies against Pride 261 


modies which they loved too much. Naught but dust and 
ashes, worms and corruption.” 

This is the inevitable fate of the human body, however 
‘enderly and delicately nurtured. Ah! Would to God that 
che evil ended here! But more terrible still is all that follows 
death: the dread tribunal of God’s justice; the sentence 
passed upon the guilty; the weeping and gnashing of teeth, 
the tortures of the worm that never dies; and the fire which 
will never be extinguished. 

Consider also the danger of vainglory, the daughter of 
pride, which as St. Bernard says, enters lightly but wounds 
deeply. Therefore, when men praise you, think whether you 
really possess the qualities for which they commend you. If 
you do not, you have no reason to be proud. But if you have 
justly merited their praise, remember the gifts of God, and 
say with the Apostle, “By the grace of God I am what I 
am.” (1 Cor. 15:10). Humble yourself, then, when you hear 
the song of praise, and refer all to the glory of God. Thus 
you will render yourself not unworthy of what He bestows 
upon you. For it is incontestable that the respect men pay 
/you, and the good for which they honor you, are due to 
|God. You rob Him, therefore, of all the merit which you 
appropriate to yourself. Can any servant be more unfaithful 
‘than one who steals his master’s glory? Consider, moreover, 
| how unreasonable it is to rate your merit by the inconstant 
‘opinion of men who today are for you, and tomorrow 
against you; who today honor you, and tomorrow revile 
you. If your merit rests upon so slight a foundation, at one 
time you will be great, at another base, and again nothing at 
all, according to the capricious variations of the minds of 
men. 

Oh, no; do not rely upon the vain commendations of 
others, but upon what you really know of yourself. Though 
‘men extol you to the skies, listen to the warnings of your 
‘conscience and accept the testimony of this intimate friend 


;

rather than the blind opinion of those who can judge you | 
only from a distance and by what they hear. Make no ac- | 
count of the judgments of men, but commit your glory to the | 
care of God, whose wisdom will preserve it for you and_ 
whose fidelity will restore it to you in the sight of angels and - 
men. | 

Be mindful also, O ambitious man, of the dangers to 
which you expose yourself by seeking to command others. 
How can you command when you have not yet learned to 
obey? How can you take upon yourself the care of others 
when you can hardly account for yourself? Consider what a 
risk you incur by adding to your own sins those of persons 
subject to your authority. Holy Scripture tells us that they 
who govern will be severely judged, and that the mighty 
shall be mightily tormented. (Cf. Wis. 6:6). Who can ex- 
press the cares and troubles of one who is placed over 
many? We read of a certain king who, on the day of his cor- 
onation, took the crown in his hands, and, gazing upon it, 
exclaimed, “O crown richer in thorns than in happiness, did 
one truly know thee he would not stoop to pick thee up even 
if he found thee lying at his feet.” 

Again, O proud man, I would ask you to remember that 
your pride is displeasing to all—to God, who resists the 
proud and gives His grace to the humble (Cf. James 4:6); to 
the humble, who hold in horror all that savors of arrogance: 
and to the proud themselves, who naturally hate all who 
claim to be greater than they. Nor will you be pleasing to 
yourself. For if it ever be given to you in this world to enter 
into yourself and recognize the vanity and folly of your life, 
you will certainly be ashamed of your littleness. And if you 
do not correct it here, still less satisfaction will it afford you 
in the next world, where it will bring upon you eternal tor- 
ments. 

St. Bernard tells us that if we truly knew our hearts we 
would be displeasing to ourselves, which alone would make

; pleasing to God; but because we do not know ourselves 
are inflated with pride and therefore hateful in His sight. 
he time will come when we shall be odious to God and to 
urselves—to God because of our crimes, and to ourselves 
cause of the punishment they will bring upon us. Our 
‘ide pleases the devil only; for as it was pride which 
sanged him from a pure and beautiful angel into a spirit of 
alice and deformity, he rejoices to find this evil reducing 
hers to his unhappy state. 
Another consideration which will help you acquire 
umility is the thought of the little you have done purely for 
od. How many vices assume the mask of virtue! How fre-

ptions which shine with dazzling splendor before men 
ve no beauty before God! The judgments of God are 
ifferent from those of men. A humble sinner is less dis-

roud can be called just. 
Nevertheless, though you have performed good works, 
0 not forget your evil deeds, which probably far exceed 
jour works of virtue, and which may be so full of faults and 
> negligently performed that you have more reason to ask

ather than confidence, after the example of holy Job, who

SECTION II 
Particular Remedies 


Since humility comes from a knowledge of ourselves, 
pride necessarily springs from ignorance of ourselves. 
Whoever, therefore, seriously desires to acquire humility 
must earnestly labor to know himself. How, in fact, can he 
be otherwise than humbled who, looking into his heart with 
the light of truth, finds himself filled with sins; defiled with 
the stains of sinful pleasures; the sport of ‘a thousand errors. 
fears, and caprices; the victim of innumerable anxieties and 
petty cares; oppressed by the weight of a mortal body; so 
forward in evil and so backward in good? Study yourself. 
then, with serious attention, and you will find in yourself 
nothing of which to be proud. 

But there are some who, though humbled at the sight of 
their failings, are nevertheless excited to pride when they 
examine the lives of others whom they consider less vir- 
tuous than themselves. Those who yield to this illusion 
ought to reflect, though they may excel their neighbors ir 
some virtues, that in others they are inferior to them 
Beware, then, lest you esteem yourself and despise your 
neighbor because you are more abstemious and industrious 
when he is probably much more humble, more patient, anc 
more charitable than you. Let your principal labor 
therefore, be to discover what you lack, and not what you 
possess. 

Study the virtues which adorn the soul of your neighbo: 
rather than those with which you think yourself endowed 
You will thus keep yourself in sentiments of humility, anc 
increase in your soul a desire for perfection. But if you keey 
your eyes fixed on the virtues, real or imaginary, which you 
possess, and regard in others only their failings, you wil 
naturally prefer yourself to them, and thus you will becom 
Satisfied with your condition and cease to make any effort 


Remedies against Pride 265 


| advance. 

if you find yourself inclined to take pride in a good ac- 
ai, carefully watch the feelings of your heart, bearing in 
tnd that this satisfaction and vainglory will destroy all the 
erit of your labor. Attribute no good to yourself, but refer 
erything to God. Repress all suggestions of pride with the 
-autiful words of the great Apostle: ‘What hast thou that 
ou hast not received? And if thou hast received, why dost 
ou glory as if thou hadst not received it?” (J Cor. 4:7). 
(hen your good works are practices of supererogation or 
prfection, unless your position requires you to give an ex- 
aple, do not let your right hand know what your left hand 
yes, for vainglory is more easily excited by good works 
p in public. 

When you feel sentiments of vanity or pride rising in your 
eart, hasten to apply a remedy immediately. One that is 
‘ost efficacious consists in recalling to mind all your sins, 
articularly the most shameful. Like a wise physician, you 
‘ill thus counteract the effect of one poison by another. Im- 
ate the peacock, and when you feel yourself inflated with 
ride turn your eyes upon your greatest deformity, and your 
anity will soon fall to the ground. The greater your posi- 
on the greater should be your humility, for there is not 
uch merit in being humble in poverty and obscurity. If 
bu know how to preserve humility in the midst of honors 

d dignities you will acquire real merit and virtue, for 

mility in the midst of greatness is the grandest accom- 

niment of honors, the dignity of dignities, without which 
ere is no true excellence. If you sincerely desire to ac- 

ire humility you must courageously enter the path of 
umiliation, for if you will not endure humiliations you will 
ever become humble. Though many are humbled without 
iminishing their pride, humiliation, as St. Bernard tells us, 
nevertheless the path to humility, as patience is the path 
) peace, and study to learning. Be not satisfied, therefore, 


266 The Sinner’s Guide 


with humbly obeying God, but be subject to all creatures 
for love of Him. (Cf. J Pet. 2:13). 

In another place St. Bernard speaks of three kinds of feai 
with which he would have us guard our hearts. “Fear,” he 
says, “when you are in possession of grace, lest you may dc 
something unworthy of it; fear when you have lost grace 
because you are deprived of a strong protection; and fea 
when you have recovered grace, lest you should again loss 
it.” Thus you will never trust to your own strength; the fea: 
of God which will fill your heart will save you fron 
presumption. 

Be patient in bearing persecution, for the patient en 
durance of affronts is the touchstone of true humility. Neve 
despise the poor and abject, for their misery should move u 
to compassion rather than contempt. Be not too eager fo 
rich apparel, for humility is incompatible with a love of dis 
play. One who is too solicitous about his dress is a slave t 
the opinions of men, for he certainly would not expend si 
much labor upon it if he thought he would not be observed 
Beware, however, of going to the other extreme and dress 
ing in a manner unsuited to your position. While claimin 
to despise the approbation or notice of the world, many sec 
retly strive for it by their singularity and exaggerate 
simplicity. Finally, do not disdain humble and obscur 
employments. Only the proud seek to avoid these, for th 
man of true humility deems nothing in the world beneat 
him. 


CHAPTER 31 
Remedies against Covetousness 


SECTION I 
Against Covetousness in General 


| Covetousness is an inordinate desire for riches. Hence we 
gard as covetous not only the man who steals, but also the 
an who passionately longs for another’s goods or too 
gerly clings to his own. With great force St. Paul con- 
emns this vice and declares it the source of all iniquity: 
They that will become rich fall into temptation and into 
e snare of the devil, and into many unprofitable and 
urtful desires, which drown men into destruction and per- 
ition; for the desire of money is the root of all evil.” (J 
im. 6:9-10). 

| When you are assailed by this vice, arm yourself with the 
lowing considerations: Remember that Our Lord and 
(oh at His coming into this world, disdained to possess 
ches, which are the object of your desires. On the con- 
ary, He so loved poverty that He chose for His Mother not 
rich and powerful queen, but a poor and humble Virgin. 
'e willed to be born, not in a palace, but in a bleak stable, 
ie manger of which, covered with a little straw, was His 
aly couch. 

During His life upon earth He never ceased to manifest 
is love for poverty and His contempt for riches. For His 
postles He chose not the princes of great houses, but poor 
ad ignorant fishermen. What greater presumption can 


267 


268 The Sinner’s Guide 


there be than that of a base worm coveting riches, when the 
Creator of the universe became so poor for love of him! 

Consider, moreover, your own vileness, since you aré 
willing for a gross and perishable interest to sacrifice you 
immortal soul, created to the image of God and redeemec 
by His Blood, compared with which the whole world is 
nothing. God would not give His life for this materia 
world, but He gave it for the soul of man. How muct 
greater, therefore, must be the value of a soul! True riche: 
do not consist in silver, or gold, or precious stones, but i1 
virtue, the inseparable companion of a good conscience. Se 
aside the vain opinions of men, and you will see that thess 
precious metals are such only by the judgment of the world 
Will you, who are a Christian, become a slave to that whict 
even pagan philosophers despised? “He who guards hi: 
riches like a slave is their victim,” says St. Jerome; “but he 
who throws off their yoke possesses them as their lord anc 
master.” 

Consider also these words of Our Saviour: “No man cat 
serve two masters, God and mammon.” (Matt. 6:24). Mat 
cannot freely rise to God and the contemplation of Hi: 
beauty while he is breathless in the pursuit of riches. / 
heart filled with material and earthly pleasures can neve 
know spiritual and divine joys. No; it is impossible to unit 
what is false with what is true; what is spiritual with what i 
carnal; what is temporal with what is eternal; they can neve 
dwell together in one heart. 

There is another truth of which you must not lose sight 
The more worldly prosperity you enjoy, the more destitut 
you are likely to be of spiritual riches, for an abundance o 
this world’s goods leads you to trust in them rather than it 
God. Oh! That you knew the misery which such prosperit 
prepares for you! The desire of more which springs fror 
the love of riches is a torment which far exceeds th 
pleasure we derive from their possession. It will entangl 


Remedies against Covetousness 269 


oa in a thousand temptations, fill you with cares, and 
ader the delusive image of pleasure plunge you into 
“aewed sin and prove an inexhaustible source of trouble 
ad disquiet. Again, riches are acquired only at the expense 
f pain and labor; they are preserved only by care and anx- 
“ty; and they are never lost without bitter vexation and 
rief. But, worse than all this, they are rarely accumulated 
rithout offence against God; for, as the proverb says, “A 
‘ch man is either a wicked man or a wicked man’s heir.” 
Moreover, all the riches of the world, did you possess 
hem, would never satisfy the desires of your heart. They 
vould only excite and increase them. However great the 
possessions you accumulate, there will be a continual void 
ithin you; you will never cease to long for more. In its pur- 
uit of worldly possessions your poor heart fruitlessly ex- 
austs itself, for it will never find content. It drinks deeply 
it the fountains of pleasure, yet its thirst is never appeased. 
its enjoyment of the possessions it has already acquired is 
estroyed by an insatiable thirst for more. Marveling at the 
rovetousness of the human heart, St. Augustine asks: 
pees is it that man is so insatiable in his desires, while 
rutes observe a measure in theirs? They seek their prey 
only when they feel the cravings of hunger, and after this is 
appeased they are satisfied and rest. But the covetousness of 
ie rich knows no limit; it is never satisfied, but is per- 
peel seeking more.” 
| Has not experience shown you also that where there are 
reat riches there are many to consume, to steal, or to 
Ber them? If you would free yourself from all the anx- 
‘ consequent on these cares, put yourself in the hands of 
God and fully confide in His providence, for He never for- 
sakes those who trust in Him. Since He has subjected man 
to the necessity of seeking food, He will not permit him to 
perish from hunger. Could God, who cares for the birds of 
the air and clothes the lilies of the field, be indifferent to the 


& 


270 The Sinner’s Guide 


necessities of one of His noblest creatures? Life is short; ev- 
ery moment brings us nearer to death. Why, then, lay up so 
much provision for so short a journey? Why burden your- 
self with so many possessions which must necessarily im-_ 
pede your progress? 

When you will have reached the end of your earthly 
pilgrimage, poor in this world’s goods, your wealth of real 
treasure will far exceed that of the covetous, whose lives 
have been spent in accumulating riches. How different will 
be the account exacted of you, and how readily you will 
part from the little you may have of the goods of earth, 
because you always esteemed them at their true value! But 
the rich and the covetou, in addition to the terrible account 
which will be required of them, will be rent with anguish at 
parting from that wealth which they loved and adored dur- 
ing life. 

Besides the reflections I have suggested, I would ask: For 
whom are you amassing these goods? Do you not know that 
you must leave this world as poor and naked as you entered 
it? (Cf. Job 1:21). Think of this, says St. Jerome, and it will 
be easy for you to despise the riches of this world. (Cf. Ad 
Paulin. in Prol. Bib.). Beware, then, lest in the pursuit of 
these you lose the treasures of eternity. 

Death will rob you of all your earthly possessions; your 
works, good and bad, will alone accompany you beyond the 
tomb. If this dread hour finds you unprepared, great will be 
your misfortune. All that remains to you will then be dis- 
tributed into three portions, your body will become the food 
of worms; your soul the victim of demons, and your wealth 
the prey of eager and perhaps ungrateful or extravagant 
heirs. Ah! Dear Christian, follow the counsel of Our 
Saviour; share your wealth with the poor, that it may be 
borne before you into the kingdom which you hope to en- 
joy. What folly to leave your treasures in a place of banish- 
ment whither you will never return, instead of sending them 


Remedies against Covetousness 271 


efore you to that country which is intended for your eter- 
+1 home! 

Again, I would remind you that God, as a wise and 
overeign Ruler, has appointed some of His children the 
‘epositaries of His power and the dispensers of His 
.enefits, to guide and maintain the others. If you are of the 
‘umber of those who from their surplus possessions must 
ontribute to the support of the poor, do you think that you 
.re justified in expending upon yourself what has been 
‘iven to you for the benefit of others? “The bread which 
you withhold,” says St. Basil, “is the food of the poor, the 
sarments you conceal should clothe the naked; the gold you 
cecumulate is the portion of the needy.” Therefore, you rob 
ihe poor whenever you refuse to succor them from your 
bundance. The riches you have received from God are 
meant to remedy human misery, not to be the instruments of 
4 bad life. Therefore, do not let your prosperity cause you 
o forget the Author of all your blessings, and let not those 
blessings be a subject of vainglory. Do not, I conjure you, 
prefer a land of exile to your true country. Do not convert 
into obstacles what is meant to aid you on your journey, and 
Ho not make of the succors of life instruments of eternal 
Heath. Be content with the condition in which God has 

laced you, bearing in mind the words of the Apostle: 
“Having food and wherewith to be covered, with these we 
ee content.” (J Tim. 6:8). 

“A servant of God,” says St. Chrysostom, “should never 
eek by his dress to gratify his vanity or indulge his flesh; 
his only object should be to comply with the necessities and 
requirements of his condition.” “‘Seek ye, therefore, first the 
kingdom of God and His justice, and all these things shall 
be added unto you.” (Matt. 6:33). 
Remember also that it is not poverty but the love of 
poverty which is a virtue. Hence all who voluntarily forsake 
wealth bear a striking resemblance to Our Saviour, who, 


2/2 The Sinner’s Guide 


being rich with the riches of God, became poor for love of 
us. They who are compelled to live in poverty, but bear it 
with patience, never coveting the wealth which is denied 
them, convert their necessity into a meritorious virtue. As” 
the poor by their poverty conform themselves to Jesus 

Christ, so the rich by their alms can conform their hearts to” 
the merciful Heart of this Divine Model, who in His lowly 
crib received not only the shepherds with their simple 

tokens of affection, but also the wise and. powerful men of 

the East, who came to lay at His feet the treasures of their 

gold and frankincense and myrrh. 

If, then, God has given you wealth, bestow it generously 
on the poor, assured that it will be laid up for you as treas- 
ure in the kingdom of Heaven; but if you waste the means 
God has given you, you must not expect to find any before 
you when you leave this life. Unless such a disposition is 
made of your possessions, how can you call them good, 
since you cannot bear them with you and enjoy them in 
your true home? Lay up, then, by a worthy use of your 
worldly wealth, a store of spiritual possessions, which alone 
are truly good, and of which, unless you freely surrender 
them, not even death can deprive you. 


SECTION II 
Against the unjust Detention 
of Another’s Goods 


In connection with the evil of which we are treating, let 
us say a few words on the sin of retaining the goods of 
another. Theft consists not only in unjustly taking what 
belongs to another, but also in unlawfully retaining it 
against the owner’s will. Our intention to restore it later will 
not suffice if we are able to do it at once, for we are obliged 
to make restitution as soon as possible. Inability to make 
immediate restitution justifies us in deferring it; while con- 


Remedies against Covetousness 273 


isued poverty, if so great as to afford us no means, excuses 
‘5 entirely, for God does not require what is impossible. 
We cannot better explain this doctrine than by the words 
-{ St. Gregory: “Remember that the riches you have 
enlawfully acquired remain in this world, but the sins you 
ommitted in obtaining them will accompany you into the 
:ext. How great is your folly, then, to leave your profit here 
snd to take only your loss with you—to afford others grat- 
fication in this world while you endure everlasting suffer- 
ngs in the world to come!” (Epist. ad Just). 

The folly of covetousness goes still further, and causes 
you to sacrifice yourself, your body and your soul, to your 
miserable possessions. You are like a man who, to save his 
coat, exposes his body to be pierced with a dagger. In what 
Hoes your conduct differ from that of Judas, if for a little 
money you will sell justice, divine grace, your soul itself? 
(The hour of death, at the latest, will compel you to make 
-estitution if you would save your soul. How incomprehen- 
sible, then, is the mad folly which prompts you to accumu- 
late your unlawful gains, and, by living in sin, confessing in 
sin, approaching the Holy Table in sin, completely deprive 
yourself of spiritual treasures which are incomparably 
superior to all the wealth of this world! Is he not devoid of 
reason who acts in this manner? Endeavor, therefore, to 
pay what you owe, even to the smallest sum, and permit no 
man to suffer by your neglect. (Cf. Deut. 24:15). Do not de- 
a the laborer’s wages. (Cf. Tob. 4:15). Do not compel 
him to seek and plead for what justly belongs to him, that he 
may not have reason to say that it was more difficult to ob- 
tain his wages than to earn them. 

If you have the duties of executor to fulfill, beware of 
defrauding departed souls of help due them, lest their ex- 
piation may be prolonged because of a neglect for which 
you must some day heavily atone. Pay your dependants 
regularly, and let your accounts be carefully kept, that they 


274 The Sinner’s Guide 


may give rise to no disputes or claims after your death. Do 
not wholly leave to those who survive you the execution of 
your last wishes, but fulfill them yourself as far as you are 
able; for if you are careless of your own affairs, how can 
you expect others to be more diligent? 

Make it a point of honor to owe no man, and you will 
thus enjoy peaceful slumbers, a quiet conscience, a con- 
tented life, and a happy death. The means of acquiring 
these precious results is to control your desires and ap- 
petites and to govern your expenditure by your income, not 
by your caprices. Our debts proceed from our ill-regulated, 
uncontrolled desires more than from our necessities, and 
consequently moderation is more profitable than the largest 
revenues. Let us be convinced that the only real riches, the 
only real treasures, are those which the Apostle bids us seek 
when he tells us to fly covetousness and pursue justice, 
godliness, faith, charity, patience, and mildness, for godli- 
ness with contentment is great gain. (Cf. / Tim. 6:6,11). Be 
contented with the position in which God has placed you. 
Man would always enjoy peace did he accept the portion 
which God gives him; but, seeking to gratify ambition or 
cupidity, which craves more than God has given him, he 
exposes himself to trouble and disquiet, for real happiness 
or success can never be known by one who strives against 
the will of God. 


CHAPTER 32 
Remedies against Lust 


SECTION I 
General Remedies 


Lust is an inordinate desire of unlawful pleasures. It is a 
ice most widely spread in the world; one that is most 
‘olent in its attacks, most insatiable in its cravings. Hence 
:. Augustine says that the severest warfare which a Chris- 
an has to maintain is that in defense of chastity, for such 
pmbats are frequent, and victories rare. 

Whenever you are assailed by this shameful vice resist it 
ith the following considerations: Remember, first, that 
nis disorder not only stains your soul, purified by the 
ood of Christ, but defiles your body, in which the thrice 
(oly Body of Christ has been placed, as in a shrine. If it 
a sacrilege to defile a material temple dedicated to 
iod’s service, what must it be to profane this living tem- 
fe which God has chosen for His dwelling? For this 


ason the Apostle tells us: “Fly fornication. Every sin 
at a man doth is without the body, but he that commit- 
rth fornication sinneth against his own body.” (J Cor. 
:18). Consider, secondly, that this deplorable vice neces- 
arily involves scandal to numerous souls and the spiritual 
ain of all who participate in your crime. This thought 
ill cause the sinner to suffer the greatest remorse at the 
our of death; for if in the Old Law God required a life 
or a life, an eye for an eye, a tooth for a tooth (Cf. Ex. 


Das 


276 The Sinner’s Guide 


21:23-24), what satisfaction can be offered Him for thé 
destruction of so many souls, purchased at the price of Hi 
Blood? 

This treacherous vice begins in pleasure, but ends in ar 
abyss of bitterness and remorse. There is nothing inte 
which man is more easily drawn, but nothing from whicl 
he is with more difficulty freed. Hence the Wise Mar 
compares an impure woman to a deep ditch, a narrow pit 
to show how easily souls fall into this vice, but with wha 
difficulty they are extricated. Man is first allured by it 
flattering aspect, but when he has assumed the sinful yoke 
and particularly when he has cast aside all shame, it re 
quires almost a miracle of grace to deliver him from hi: 
degrading bondage. For this reason it is justly comparec 
to a fisherman’s net, which the fish easily enter, but fron 
which they rarely escape. Learn, too, how many sin: 
spring from this one vice; for during this long captivity o 
the soul how often is God offended by thoughts, words 
and desires, if not by actions? 

The evils which it brings in its train are no les 
numerous than the sins it occasions. It robs man of hi 
reputation—his most important possession, for there is n 
vice more degrading or more shameful. It rapidly under 
mines the strength, exhausts the energy, and withers th 
beauty of its victim, bringing upon him the most foul anc 
loathsome diseases. It robs youth of its freshness, and hur 
ries it into a premature and dishonorable old age. I 
penetrates even to the sanctuary of the soul, darkening th 
understanding, obscuring the memory, and weakening th 
will. It turns man from every noble and honorable work 
burying him so deeply in the mire of his impurities that hi 
can neither think nor speak of anything but what is vile. 

Nor are the ravages of this vice confined only to ma: 
himself. They extend to all his possessions. There is n 
revenue so great that the exactions and follies of impurit 


Remedies against Lust ZU 


ii not exhaust; for it is closely allied to gluttony, and 
ese two vices combine to ruin their victim. Men given to 
‘purity are generally addicted to intemperance, and 
yander their substance in rich apparel and sumptuous 
‘ing. Moreover, their impure idols are insatiable in their 
‘mands for costly jewels, rich adornments, rare per- 
mes, which gifts they love much better than they love 
e donors, their unfortunate victims. The example of the 
‘odigal son, exhausting his inheritance in these 
asures, shows how terrible is such a passion. 

Consider, further, that the more you indulge in these in- 
mous gratifications, the more insatiable will be your 
‘sire for them, the less they will satisfy you. It is the 
ture of these pleasures to excite the appetite rather than 
pease it. If you consider how fleeting is the pleasure and 
»w enduring its punishment, you will not for a moment’s 
ijoyment sacrifice the unspeakable treasure of a good 
pnscience in this life and the eternal happiness of Heaven 
the next. St. Gregory, therefore, has truly said that the 
leasure is momentary, but the suffering is eternal. 
oral. 9,44). 

Consider also the nobility and the value of virginal 
rity, which this vice destroys. Virgins begin here below 
live as angels, for the beauty of these glorious spirits is 
flected in the splendor of their chastity. “Living in the 
sh,” says St. Bernard, “‘and despising its allurements is 
ore angelic than human.” (Un Nat. Virg.). 

“Virginity,” says St. Jerome, “is the virtue which, amid 
e corruption of this mortal life, best represents the per- 
ction of immortal glory. It brings before us the happy 
pndition of the celestial City, where there is no marrying, 
id gives us a foretaste of eternal joy.” (De Virginitatis 
aude). Hence virginity is specially rewarded in Heaven. 
. John tells us that virgins follow the Lamb whither- 
ever He goeth. (Cf. Apoc. 14:4). They have risen above 


278 The Sinner’s Guide 


their fellow men in their imitation of Christ. They will 
therefore be more closely united to Him for all eternity, 
and will find in the spotless purity of their bodies a source 
of ineffable joy. 

Virginity not only renders man like unto Christ, but 
makes him the temple of the Holy Spirit. For this Divine 
Lover of purity abhors whatever is defiled, and delights to 
dwell in chaste souls. The Son of God, who was conceived — 
of the Holy Ghost, so loved purity that He wrought His 
greatest miracle to preserve the purity of His Virgin 
Mother. If you have suffered the loss of this beautiful vir- 
tue, learn from the temptations which wrought the evil to 
guard against a second fall. 

If you have not preserved the gift of chastity in the per- 
fection in which God gave it to you, endeavor to restore 
the beauty of the Creator’s work by giving yourself to His 
service with a zeal and fervor born of deep gratitude for 
forgiven sin, and with an ardent desire to repair the past. 
“It often happens,” says St. Gregory, “that one who was 
tepid and indifferent before his fall becomes, through re- 
pentance, a strong and fervent soldier of Christ.” (Past., p 
1). Finally, since God continued to preserve your life after 
you had so basely offended Him, profit by this benefit to 
serve Him and make reparation for your sins, lest another 
fall should be irremediable. 


SECTION II 
Particular Remedies 


Besides these general remedies there are others more 
special, and perhaps more efficacious. The first of these is 
vigorously to resist the first attacks of this vice. If we do 
not resist it in the beginning, it rapidly acquires strength 
and gains an entrance to our souls. “When a taste for sin- 
ful pleasures,” says St. Gregory, “takes possession of a 


Remedies against Lust 279 


ieart, it thinks of nothing but how to gratify its inordinate 
jesires.” (Moral. 21,7). We must, then, struggle against it 
om the beginning by repelling every bad thought, for by 
uch fuel is the flame of impurity fed. As wood nourishes 
ire, so our thoughts nourish our desires; and, conse- 
yuently, if the former be good, charity will burn in our 
preast—but if they are bad, the fire of lust will certainly 
be kindled. 

In the second place, we must carefully guard our senses, 
articularly the eyes, that they may not rest upon anything 
tapable of exciting sinful desires. A man may inflict a 
eep wound upon his soul by inconsiderately turning his 
ryes upon a dangerous object. Prudently guard your eyes 
i your intercourse with the other sex, for such glances 
weaken virtue. 

Hence we are told by the Holy Ghost: “Look not round 
sbout thee in the ways of the city. Turn away thy face 
irom a woman dressed up, and gaze not upon another’s 
peauty.”” (Ecclus. 9:7-8). Think of Job, that great servant 
( God, of such tried virtue, who kept so vigilant a guard 
pver his senses that, in the expressive language of Scrip- 
ure, he made a covenant with his eyes not so much as to 
ink upon a virgin. (Cf. Job 31:1). Behold also the exam- 
ble of David, who, though declared by God to have been a 
man after His own Heart, yet fell into three grievous 
primes by inconsiderately looking upon a woman. 
| Be no less watchful in protecting your ears from impure 
liscourses. If unbecoming words are uttered in your pres- 
ce, testify your displeasure by at least a grave and 
rious countenance; for what we hear with pleasure we 
arn to do with complacency. Guard with equal care your 
ongue. Let no immodest words escape you; for “evil com- 
munications,” says the Apostle, ‘corrupt good morals.” (/ 
Sor. 15:33). A man’s conversation discovers his inclina- 
ion, for, to quote the words of the Gospel, from the abun- 


280 The Sinner’s Guide 


dance of the heart the mouth speaketh. 

Endeavor to keep your mind occupied with good 
thoughts and your body employed in some profitable exer- 
cise, “for the devil,” says St. Bernard, “fills idle souls with 
bad thoughts, so that they may be thinking of evil if they 
do not actually commit it.” | 

In all temptations, but particularly in temptations 
against purity, remember the presence of your guardian 
angel and of the devil, your accuser, for they both witness. 
all your actions, and will render an account of them to 
Him who sees and judges all things. If you follow this 
counsel, how can you, before your accuser, your defender, 
and your Judge, commit a base sin, for which you would 
blush before the lowest of men? Remember also the terri- 
ble tribunal of God’s judgment and the eternal flames of 
Hell; for as a greater pain makes us insensible to a less, so 
the thought of the inexhaustible fire of Hell will render us 
insensible to the fire of concupiscence. 

In addition to all this, be very guarded in your inter- 
course with women, and beware of continuing alone with 
one for any length of time; for, according to St. 
Chrysostom, the enemy attacks men and women more 
vigorously when he finds them alone. He is bolder when 
there are no witnesses present to thwart his artifices. 
Avoid the society of women who are not above suspicion, 
for their words inflame the heart, their glances wound the 
soul, and everything about them is a snare to those who 
visit them with imprudent familiarity. Be mindful of the 
example of the elders (Cf. Dan. 13), and let not old age 
render you less prudent. Do not trust to your own strength; 
and let not a habit of virtue inspire you with 
presumptuous confidence. Let there be no improper in- 
terchange of presents, visits, or letters, for these are so 
many snares which entangle us and reawaken dangerous 
affections. If you experience any friendship for a virtuous 


Remedies against Lust 281 


voman let your intercourse be marked by grave respect, 
ind avoid seeing her too often or conversing too familiarly 
vith her. But, as one of the most important remedies is 
svoiding dangerous occasions, we shall give an example 
rom the Dialogues of St. Gregory to show you with what 
srudence holy souls guard this angelic virtue. 
There lived in the province of Mysia a holy priest who 
vas filled with the fear of God, and who governed his 
church with zeal and wisdom. A very virtuous woman had 
charge of the altar and church furniture. This holy soul 
the priest loved as a sister, but he was as guarded in his 
intercourse with her as if she were his enemy. He never 
ermitted her to approach him or converse familiarly with 
him, or enter his dwelling, thus removing all occasions of 
familiarity; for the saints not only reject unlawful grat- 
ifications, but forbid themselves even innocent pleasures 
hen there is the slightest indication of danger to the soul. 
For this reason the good priest would never allow her to 
minister to him, even in his extreme necessities. 
At an advanced age, after he had been 40 years in the 
acred ministry, he fell gravely ill, and was soon almost at 
he point of death. As he lay in this condition, the good 
woman, wishing to discover whether he still lived, bent 
ver him and put her ear to his mouth to listen to his 
breathing. The dying man, perceiving her, indignantly ex- 
laimed, ‘‘Get thee hence, woman! Get thee hence! The 
fire still glows in the embers. Beware of kindling it with 
straw!’ As she withdrew he seemed to gain new strength, 
and raising his eyes, he cried out with a loud voice, “Oh! 
Happy hour! Welcome, my lords, welcome! I thank you 
for deigning to visit so poor a servant. I come! I come!” 
He repeated these words several times, and when they who 
were present asked him to whom he spoke, he said with 
astonishment, “Do you not see the glorious Apostles St. 
Peter and St. Paul?” And, raising his eyes, he again cried, 


| 


282 The Sinner’s Guide 


“I come! I come!” and as he uttered these words he gave 
up his soul to God. 

An end so glorious was the result of a prudent vigilance 
which cannot be too highly extolled; and such confidence 
at the hour of death seemed a fitting reward for one who 
during life had been filled with a holy fear of God. (Dial. 
4,11). 


CHAPTER 33 


e Remedies against Envy 


Envy consists in grieving at another’s good or repining 
\t another’s happiness. The envious man looks with hatred 
kpon his superiors who excel him, upon his equals who 
ompete with him, upon his inferiors who strive to equal 
iim. Saul’s envy of David and the Pharisees’ envy of 
Christ could only be satisfied by death; for it is the 
-haracter of this cruel vice to stop at nothing until it has 
-ompassed its end. Of its nature it is a mortal sin, because, 
ee hatred, it is directly opposed to charity. However, in 
his, as in other sins, there are degrees which do not con- 
titute a mortal sin, as, for example, when hatred or envy 
s not grave, or when the will does not fully consent. 
| Envy is a most powerful, a most injurious vice. It is 
as all over the world, but predominates particularly in 
he courts of kings and in the society of the rich and 
yowerful. Who, then, can be free from its attacks? Who is 
( fortunate as to be neither the slave nor the object of 
nvy? From the beginning of the world history abounds 
with examples of this fatal vice. It was the cause of the 
pes ie which stained the earth, when Cain killed 
. (Cf. Gen. 4). It existed between the brothers 
Wcics and Remus, the founders of Rome, and the latter 
Fell a victim to the envy of the former. Behold its effects in 
he brothers of Joseph, who sold him as a slave. (Cf. Gen. 
37); in Aaron and Mary, the brother and sister of Moses. 


283 


284 The Sinner’s Guide 


(Cf. Num. 12). Even the disciples of Our Lord, before the 
coming of the Holy Ghost, were not wholly free from it. 

Ah! When we see such examples, what must we expect 
to find among worldlings, who are far from ‘possessing 
such sanctity, and who are seldom bound to one another 
by any ties? Nothing can give us an idea of the power of 
this vice or the ravages it effects. Good men are its natural 
prey, for it attacks with its poisoned dart all virtue and all 
talent. Hence Solomon says that all the labors and indus- 
tries of men are exposed to the envy of their neighbors. 
(Cf. Eccles. 4:4). 

Therefore, you must diligently arm yourself against the 
attacks of such an enemy, and unceasingly ask God to 
deliver you from it. Let your efforts against it be firm and 
constant. If it persevere in its attacks, continue to oppose 
an obstinate resistance, and make little account of the un- 
worthy sentiments it suggests. If your neighbor enjoys a 
prosperity which is denied you, thank God for it, per- 
suaded that you have not merited it or that it would not be 
salutary for you. Remember, moreover, that envying the 
prosperity of others does not alleviate your own misery, 
but rather increases it. 

To strengthen your aversion to this vice, make use of 
the following reflections: Consider, first, what a 
resemblance the envious man bears to the devils, who look 
with rage upon our good works and the heavenly reward 
we are to receive for them. They have no hope of the hap- 
piness of which they would deprive us, for they know that 
they have irretrievably lost it; but they are unwilling that 
beings created out of dust should enjoy honors of which 
they have been dispossessed. For this reason St. Augustine 
says, “May God preserve from this vice not only the 
hearts of all Christians, but of all men, for it is the special 
vice of devils, and one which causes them the most hope- 
less suffering.” The crime of Satan is not theft or impurity, 


Remedies against Envy 285 


ut enviously seeking, after his fall, to make man imitate 
is rebellion. This is truly the feeling which actuates the 
hivious. 

Oftentimes the prosperity of others is no prejudice to 
iem; they could not profit by what they strive to take 
‘om their neighbor; but they would have all equally 
1iserable with themselves. If, then, the possessions which 
ou envy in another could not be yours were he dis- 
ossessed of them, why should they be a cause of grief to 
ou? When you envy the virtue of another you are your 
wn greatest enemy; for if you continue in a state of grace, 
nited to your neighbor through charity, you have a share 
1 all his good works, and the more he merits the richer 
jou become. So far, therefore, from envying his virtue, 
jou should find it a source of consolation. Alas! Because 
jour neighbor is advancing, will you fall back? Ah! If you 
vould love in him the virtues which you do not find in 
ourself, you would share in them through charity; the 
brofit of his labors would also become yours. 

Consider, moreover, how envy corrodes the heart, 
veakens the understanding, destroys all peace of soul, and 
jondemns us to a melancholy and intolerable existence. 
Like the worm which eats the wood in which it is engen- 
.ered, it preys upon the heart in which it was given birth. 
ts ravages extend even to the countenance, whose pale- 
less testifies to the passion which rages within. This vice 
3 itself the severest judge against its victim, for the en- 
ious man is subjected to its severest tortures. Hence cer- 
ain authors have termed it a just vice, not meaning that it 
s good, for it is a most heinous sin, but meaning that it is 
is Own greatest punishment. 

Consider, again, how opposed is the sin of envy to 
harity, which is God, and to the common good, which 
veryone should promote to the best of his ability; for 
vhen we envy another’s good, when we hate those to 


286 The Sinner’s Guide 


whom God unceasingly manifests His love, when we per- 
secute those whom He created and redeemed, do we not, 
at least in desire, strive to undo the work of God? 

But a more efficacious remedy against this vice is to 
love humility and abhor pride, which is the father of envy. 
A proud man, who cannot brook a superior or an equal, 
naturally envies all who appear to excel him, persuading 
himself that he descends in proportion as another rises. 
Hence the Apostle says, “Let us not be desirous of vain 
glory, provoking one another, envying one another.” (Gal. 
5:26). In other words, let us destroy the root of envy, 
which is vainglory. 

Let us wean our hearts from worldly honors and posses- 
sions, and seek only spiritual riches, for such treasures are 
not diminished when enjoyed by numbers, but, on the con- 
trary, are increased. It is otherwise with the goods of the 
earth, which must decrease in proportion to the numbers 
who share them. For this reason envy finds easy access to 
the soul which covets the riches of this life, where one 
necessarily loses what another gains. 

Do not be satisfied with feeling no grief at the 
prosperity of your neighbor, but endeavor to benefit him 
all you can, and the good you cannot give him ask God to 
grant him. Hate no man. Love your friends in God, and 
your enemies for God. He so loved you while you were 
still His enemy that He shed the last drop of His Blood to 
save you from the tyranny of your sins. 

Your neighbor may be wicked, but that is no reason fot 
hating him. In such a case imitate the example of a wise 
physician, who loves his patient, but hates his disease. We 
must abhor sin, which is the work of man, but we must al. 
ways love our neighbor, who is the work of God. Never 
say in your heart: “What is my neighbor to me? I owe hin 
nothing. We are bound by no ties of blood or interest. He 
has never done me a favor, but has probably injured me.’ 


Remedies against Envy 287 


‘flect rather on the benefits which God unceasingly 
stows upon you, and remember that all He asks in 
urn is that you be charitable and generous, not to Him, 
: He has no need of you or your possessions, but to your 
ighbor, whom He has recommended to your love.

Gluttony is an inordinate love of eating and drinking

Consider also Our Saviour’s extraordinary fast in th

then, can you call yourself a follower of Christ, if, whe 
He fasts, you abandon yourself to the gross pleasures « 
the table? He refuses no labor, no suffering, to redeet

If you find abstinence difficult, think of the gall an

egar which were given to Our Saviour on the cross; for 
S:. Bernard tells us, there is no food so unpleasant that 
‘ay not be made palatable by mingling it with this bit- 
‘draught. Frequently reflect upon the terrible austerities

tisfy the sensuality of the great. It is deplorable to think

f body and soul to eternal suffering. What in- 
mprehensible folly to flatter with such delicate care a 
‘dy which is destined to be the food of worms! For this 
serable body you neglect your soul, which will appear 
fore the tribunal of God as poor in virtues as its earthly 
mpanion is rich in sensual pleasures. Nor will the body 
cape the punishment to which the soul will be con- 
mned. Having been created for the soul, it will share its 
fferings. Thus by neglecting the nobler part of your 


290 The Sinner’s Guide 


being to devote yourself to the inferior, you lose both an 
become your own executioner. 

To excite in your heart a salutary fear of this vice 
recall to mind what is related in the Gospel of Lazarus, o 
his poverty, of his hunger which craved the crumbs whicl 
fell from the rich man’s table, and how he was carried bi 
angels to Abraham’s bosom; while the rich man, who fe

was buried in the depths of Hell. Moderation and glut

in the next world. To patient suffering will succeed ineffa 
ble happiness, and sensual pleasures will be followed b 
eternal misery. What remains to you now of the pleasure 
of your guilty excesses? Nothing but remorse of cons 
cience, which will be the principal torture of the life t 
come. All that you have lavished upon your ungoverne 
appetite you have irrevocably lost, but that which yo 
have given away to the poor is still yours, for its merit i 
laid up in the kingdom of Heaven. 

That you may not be deceived by the snares of this vic

submission will be the rule and foundation of its empire 
Let God command our reason; let reason direct the sou 
and the soul will be able to govern the body. By observin 
this wise order decreed by the Creator, the whole man wi 
be reformed. But when the soul rebels against reason, an 
reason against God, the body will soon rebel against th 
soul. 

If tempted by gluttony, remember that you have alread 
tasted its pleasures and that they endured but a momen 
They passed like a dream, except that while the light « 
day dispels the images of the night, the remorse for glu 
tony remains long after its pleasure has departed. Bi

2rcome this enemy, and you will experience consolation 
i peace. Therefore, the following wise saying has justly 
ome celebrated: “If you find difficulty in the perfor-

~ virtue remains; but if you take pleasure in committing 
vase action, its pleasure disappears, but its shame con- 
ues with you.” (Aul. Gel., Noct. Attic. 8,15). 


CHAPTER 35 


Remedies against Anger and Hatred 


Anger is an inordinate desire of revenge. Against th 
vice the Apostle strongly speaks: “Let all bitterness an 
anger, and indignation and clamor, and blasphemy be pt 
away from you, with all malice. And be ye kind one t 
another, merciful, forgiving one another, even as God hat 
forgiven you in Christ.” (Eph. 4:31-32). And Our Saviot 
Himself tells us: “Whosoever is angry with his brother sha 
be in danger of the judgment. And whosoever shall say 
thou fool, shall be in danger of hell fire.” (Matt. 5:22). 

When this furious enemy assails you, let the followin 
considerations help you overcome its movements: Cor 
sider, first, that even beasts live at peace with their kin 
Elephants do not war upon one another; sheep live peace 
ably in one fold; and cattle go together in herds. We see th 
cranes taking by turns the place of guard at night. Stork 
stags, dolphins, and other creatures do the same. Who dog 
not know of the friendship between the ants and the bee: 
Even the wildest animals live united among themselve 
One lion is rarely known to attack another, neither will 
tiger devour one of his kind. Yes, even the infernal spirit 
the first authors of all discord, are united in a common pu 
pose—the perversion of mankind. Man alone, for whor 
peace is most fitting, lives at enmity with his fellow men an 
indulges in implacable hatred. All animals are born wit 
weapons for combat. The bull has horns; the boar has tusk 


292, 


Remedies against Anger and Hatred 293 


he bird has.a beak and claws; the bee has a sting, and even 
he tiny fly or other insect has power to bite. But man, 
‘estined to live at peace with his fellow creatures, comes 
to the world naked and unarmed. Reflect, then, how con- 
rary to your rightful nature it is to seek to be revenged 
‘oon one of your kind, to return evil for evil, particularly 
sy making use of weapons which nature has denied you. 
In the second place, a thirst for vengeance is a vice which 
nefits only savage beasts. You belie your origin, you dis- 
brace your descent, when you indulge in ungovernable 
jage, worthy only of a wild animal. AElian tells of a lion 
jhat had been wounded by an African in a mountain defile. 
A year after, when this man passed the same way in the 
suite of King Juba, the lion, recognizing him, rushed among 
he royal guards, and, before he could be restrained, fell 
pon his enemy and tore him to pieces. Such is the model of 
jhe angry, vindictive man. Instead of calming his fierce rage 
y the power of reason, that noble gift which he shares with 
he angels, he abandons himself to the blind impulse of pas- 
sions which he possesses in common with the brutes. 
If it be hard to subdue your anger, excited by an injury 
rom one of your fellow creatures, consider how much more 
od has borne from you and how much He has endured for 

ou. Were you not His enemy when He shed the last drop of 

is Blood for you? And behold with what sweetness and 
patience He bears with your daily offenses against Him, 
in with what mercy and tenderness He receives you when 

ou return to Him. 

If anger urges that your enemy does not deserve forgive- 

ness, ask yourself how far you have merited God’s pardon. 

ill you have God exercise only mercy toward you, when 
you pursue your neighbor with implacable hatred? And if it 
be true that your enemy does not deserve pardon from you, 
it will be equally true that you do not deserve pardon from 
God. Remember that the pardon which man has not 


294 The Sinner’s Guide 


merited for himself, Christ has superabundantly merited for 
him. For love of Him, therefore, forgive all who have of- | 
fended you. 

Be assured, moreover, that as long as hatred predomi- 
nates in your heart you can make no offering which will be | 
acceptable to God, who has said: “If thou offer thy gift at 
the altar, and there thou remember that thy brother hath 
anything against thee, leave there thy offering before the 
altar, and go first to be reconciled to thy brother, and then 
coming thou shalt offer thy gift.” (Matt. 5:23-24). Hence 
you can realize how grievous is the sin of enmity among 
men, since it causes an enmity between God and us, and 
destroys the merit of all our good works. “We gain no merit 
from good works,” says St. Gregory, “if we have not 
learned to endure injuries with patience.”’ (Moral. 21:16). 

Consider also that the fellow creature whom you hate is 
either a just man or a sinner. If a just man, it is certainly a 
great misfortune to be the declared enemy of a friend of 
God. If a sinner, it is no less deplorable that you should un- 
dertake to punish the malice of another by plunging your 
own soul into sin. And if your neighbor in his turn seeks 
vengeance for the injury you inflict upon him, where will 
your enmities end? Will there be any peace on the earth? 

The Apostle teaches us a more noble revenge when he 
tells us “not to be overcome by evil, but to overcome evil by 
good” (Rom. 12:21)—that is, to triumph by our virtues 
over the vices of our brethren. In endeavoring to be 
revenged upon a fellow creature you are often disappointed 
and vanquished by anger itself. But if you overcome your 
passion, you gain a more glorious victory than he who con- 
quers a city. Our noblest triumph is won by subduing our- 
selves, by subjecting our passions to the empire of reason. 

Besides these, reflect on the fatal blindness into which 
this passion leads man. Under the cover of justice or right, 
how often does it drive him to excesses which cause him a 


Remedies against Anger and Hatred 295 


liclong remorse! 

The most efficacious, the sovereign remedy against this 
‘ce is to pluck from your heart inordinate love of self and 
£ everything that pertains to you. Otherwise the slightest 
vord or action directed against you or your interests will 
love you to anger. The more you are inclined to this vice 
he more persevering you should be in the practice of pa- 
ience. Accustom yourself, as far as you can, calmly to face 
1e contradictions and disappointments you are likely to en- 
-ounter, and their effect upon you will thus be greatly 
iminished. 

Make a firm resolution never to speak or act under the in- 
‘uence of anger, nor to heed any suggestions, however 
ee which your heart may urge at such moments. 

ever act until your anger has subsided, or until you have 
once or twice repeated the Our Father or some other 
prayer. Plutarch tells of a wise man who, on taking leave of 
4 monarch, advised him never to speak or act in anger, but 
.o wait until he had repeated to himself the letters of the 
alphabet. Learn a lesson from this, and avoid the evil con- 
sequences of acting from the impulse of anger. 

Though there is no time more unfavorable for action, yet 
there i is no time in which we feel ourselves more strongly 
impelled to act than when in anger. This is an additional 
reason for opposing, with all our strength, the suggestions 
of this passion. For as a man intoxicated with wine is in- 
capable of acting according to reason, and afterwards re- 
pents of what he has done in such a condition, so a man 
beside himself with passion, intoxicated with anger, is in- 
capable of any action of which he will not repent in his 
calmer moments. Anger, ea and sensuality are evil coun- 
selors. “Wine and women,” says Solomon, “make wise men 
fall off.” (Ecclus. 19:2). By wine he means not only the li- 
quor which stupefies the intellect, but all violent passion 
which blinds the judgment. Bear in mind also that you are 


296 The Sinner’s Guide 


held responsible for sins committed in such a state. 

Another very salutary remedy is to turn your thoughts to 
other things when excited to anger, and to endeavor to 
banish from your mind the subject which irritates you; for if 
you take away the fuel of a fire the flame soon expires. En- 
deavor also to love him with whom you are forced to be for- 
bearing, for patience which is not accompanied with love, 
being only exterior, is often changed into hatred. Hence, 
when the Apostle tells us that charity is patient, he im- 
mediately adds that it is kind (Cf. J Cor. 13:4); for true 
charity loves those whom it patiently endures. Finally, if 
you have excited the anger of your neighbor, quietly with- 
draw until his passion has subsided, or at least answer him 
with mildness, for “‘a mild answer breaketh wrath.” (Prov. 
1571): 


CHAPTER 36 


Remedies against Sloth 


Sloth is a reluctance to attend to duty, and, according to 
Cassian, it is especially a weariness or distate for spiritual 
| things. The peril to which this vice exposes us is clearly set 
forth in these words of Our Saviour: “Every tree that 
bringeth not forth good fruit shall be cut down and shall be 
‘cast into the fire.” (Matt. 7:19). Against its evil effects He 
| again warned His disciples when, exhorting them to 
| diligence, the opposite of sloth, He told them to watch and 
pray, for they knew not when the Lord of the house would 

come. (Cf. Mk. 13:35). 
| Therefore, if this shameful vice attack you, banish it by 
the thoughts we are about to suggest. 

First call to mind the extraordinary labors which Our 
- Lord endured for you; the many sleepless nights He spent in 
_ prayer for you; His weary journeys from city to city, healing 
the sick, comforting the sorrowful, and raising the dead. 
_ How ardently, how unceasingly He devoted Himself to the 
_ work of our redemption! Consider particularly how, at the 
~ time of His Passion, He bore upon His bruised and bleeding 

shoulders the heavy weight of His cross for love of you. If 
the God of majesty labored thus to deliver you, will you 
refuse to cooperate in your own salvation? When this tender 
Lamb endured such rude labors to free you from your sins, 
will you endure nothing to expiate them? Remember, too, 
the weary labors of the Apostles, who preached the Gospel 


297 


298 The Sinner’s Guide 


* to the whole world. Think of the sufferings endured by the 
martyrs, confessors, virgins, anchorites, and by all who are 
now reigning with Christ. It was by their teaching and their 
toil that the Faith of Christ spread through the known world 
and that the Church has been perpetuated to the present 
day. 

* Turn your eyes towards nature, and you will find nothing 
idle. The heavens, by their perpetual motion, unceasingly 
proclaim the glory of their Creator. The sun, moon, and 
stars, with all the brilliant planets which péople almost in- 
finite space, daily follow their courses for the benefit of 
man. The growth of plants and trees is continual until they 
have attained their appointed strength and proportions. 
Behold the untiring energy with which the ant labors for its 
winter’s food; with which the bees toil in building their 
hives and storing them with honey. These industrious little 
creatures will not allow an idler to exist among them; the 
drones are all killed. Throughout nature you find the same 
lesson. 

Will not man, therefore, blush for a vice which the in- 
stinct of irrational creatures teaches them to avoid? To 
what labor do not men condemn themselves for the acquisi- 
tion of perishable riches, the preservation of which, when 
they are obtained, is an ever-increasing source of care and 
anxiety! You are striving for the kingdom of Heaven. Will 
you show less energy, will you be less diligent, in toiling for 
spiritual treasures, which can never be taken from you? 

- — If you will not profit by time and strength to labor now,a 
day will come when you will vainly seek these present op- 
portunities. Sad experience tells us how many have thus 
been disappointed. Life is short, and obstacles to good 
abound. Do not, therefore, let the promptings of sloth cause 
you to lose advantages which will never return, for “the 
night cometh when no man can work.” (Jn. 9:4). 

- The number and enormity of your sins demand a propor- 


Remedies against Sloth 299 


enate penance and fervor to satisfy for them. St. Peter 
enied his Master three times, but never ceased to weep for 
His sin, though he knew it had been pardoned. St. Mary 
{agdalen to the end of her life likewise bewailed the disor- 
ers of her youth, though she heard from Our Saviour’s lips 
hese sweet words: “Thy sins are forgiven thee.’’ Numerous 
re the examples of those who, returning to God, continued 
juring life to do penance for their sins, though many of 
nem had offended God far less grievously than you. 
You daily heap up your sins; and can you consider any 
abor too severe to expiate them? Oh! Profit by this time of 
‘race and mercy to bring forth fruits worthy of penance, 
ind by the labors of this life to purchase the eternal repose 
»f the next. Our works in themselves are paltry and insig- 
uificant, but united to the merits of Christ they acquire in- 
nite value in the sight of God. The labor endures but a 
1 time; the reward will continue for eternity. We are 
ld of a saint who was wont to exclaim at the striking of the 
lock: “O my God! Another hour has flown—one of those 
‘ours sent me in which to work out my salvation, and for 
vhich I must render an account to Thee.” Let his example 
spire us with a determination to profit by the time which 
p given us to lay up works for eternal life. 
_If overwhelmed with labors, remember that we must 
nter Heaven by the way of tribulation, and that he only 
vill be crowned who strives lawfully. (Cf. 2 Tim. 2:5). If 
to abandon the struggle, remember that it is writ- 
: “He that shall persevere unto the end, he shall be 
i oe ” (Matt. 10:22). Without this perseverance, our labor 
vill neither bear fruit nor merit reward. Our Saviour would 
jot descend from the cross when asked by the Jews, for the 
vork of our redemption was not yet accomplished. If, then, 
e desire to follow in the footsteps of our Divine Model, let 
s labor to the end with unwearied zeal. Is not the reward 
yhich awaits us eternal? Let us continue to do penance; let 


300 The Sinner’s Guide 


us carry our cross after Christ. What will it avail us to have 
weathered the storms and triumphed over the perils of the 
sea of life, if we suffer shipwrecks as we are about to enter 
the port of eternal rest? 

Let not the duration or difficulty of the labors alarm you. 
God, who calls you to combat, will give you victory. He 
sees your weakness; He will support you when you falter, 
and He will reward you when you conquer. Reanimate your 
failing courage, not by comparing the difficulties of virtue 
with the pleasures of vice, but by comparing the labor 
which precedes virtue with the trouble which surely follows 
vice. Place side by side the fleeting pleasure of sin and the 
eternal happiness of virtue, and you will see how preferable 
is God’s service to the fatal repose to which sloth allures 
you. 

Yet do not allow victory to render you indolent, for suc- 
cess often lulls us into a dangerous confidence. Never aban- 
don your arms; for your enemies never sleep, and life with- 
out temptations is as impossible as a sea of perpetual calm. 
A man is usually tempted most at the beginning of a good 
life, for the devil has no need to tempt those who have aban- 
doned themselves to his control. But he is unceasing in his 
efforts against those who have resolved to give themselves 
to God. Therefore, let him never find you unprepared, but, 
like a soldier in an enemy’s country, be always ready for 
combat. 

If you are sometimes wounded, beware of throwing away 
your arms and surrendering in dismay. Rather, imitate 
those brave warriors whom the shame of defeat spurs to 
more heroic resistance and greater deeds of valor. Thus you 
will rise from a fall with new strength. You will see the 
enemy to whom you were formerly submitted now flying 
before you. And if, as it may happen in battle, you are 
repeatedly wounded, do not lose heart, but remember that 
the valor of a soldier does not consist in escaping wounds. 


Remedies against Sloth 301 


:t In never surrendering. We do not call a combatant de- 
aied when he is covered with wounds, but when he loses 
wrage and abandons the field. And when you are 
»unded lose no time in applying a remedy; for one wound 
more easily cured than two, and a fresh wound more 
sickly than one that has been inflamed by neglect. Do not 
: Satisfied with resisting temptation, but gather from it 
eater incentives to virtue, and with the assistance of God’s 
ace you will reap profit rather than harm from the attacks 
the enemy. 
lif you are tempted to gluttony or sensuality, retrench 
|mething from your usual repasts, even though they in no 
ly exceed the limits of sobriety, and give yourself with

& are assailed by avarice, increase the amount of your 
ms and the number of your good works. If you feel the

amiliations. Then, perhaps, the devil may fear to tempt 
»u, seeing that you convert his snares into occasions of vir-
