od. Above all things fly idleness. Even in your hours of 
laxation do not be wholly unoccupied. And, on the other 
a do not be so absorbed in your labors that you cannot 
pm time to time raise your heart to God and treat with 
im in prayer. 


CHAPTER 37 
Other Sins to be avoided 


SECTION I 
On Taking the Name of God in Vain 


Besides the seven capital sins of which we have beer 
treating, there are others which a good Christian shoulc 
avoid with equal diligence. 

The first is taking God’s name in vain. This sin directly 
attacks the majesty of God and is more grievous than any of 
which we could be guilty against our neighbor. And this i 
true not only when we swear by God’s holy name, but wher 
we swear by the cross, by the saints, or by our own salva 
tion. Any of these oaths, if taken falsely, is a mortal sin 
Holy Scripture frequently speaks of the heinousness of suck 
offenses against God. It is true that if one swears inadver 
tently to what is false the offense is not a mortal sin, whick 
requires the full knowledge of the intellect and the full as 
sent of the will. But this restriction does not apply to thos 
who have a habit of confirming their statements by careles 
oaths without making any effort to correct themselves 
Those who swear in this way, without weighing the impor 
of their words, are culpable for this very negligence. No 
will it avail them to urge that the intention of swearing t 
what is false was furthest from their thoughts. They per 
severe in a bad habit without any attempt to overcome it 
and therefore they must bear its consequences. 

A Christian, if he would not constantly expose himself t 


302 


Other Sins to be avoided 303 


» guilt of mortal sin, should earnestly endeavor to con- 
ter a habit so pernicious. To this end let him follow the 
wasel given us by Our Saviour, and which St. James 
seats in these words: ‘‘Above all things, my brethren, 
fear not, neither by heaven, nor by the earth, nor by any 
ver oath. But let your speech be, yea, yea; no, no; that you 
i not under judgment.” (James 5:12). By these words we 
> taught the danger of contracting a habit of careless 
earing which may eventually lead us to swear falsely, and 
| to fall under the sentence of eternal death. Swearing in 
uth, judgment, and justice” (Jer. 4:2), as the prophet 
clares, is the only swearing that is justifiable. That is, we 
ould swear only to what is true in a just cause, and with 
liiberation. 
But we should not be satisfied with merely shunning the 
re of taking God’s name in vain; we should excite a hor- 
¢ of it in our children and servants, and reprove it 
nenever we encounter it. If at times we inadvertently fall 
+O it, we should impose upon ourselves some penance of a 
yer, or an alms, not only to punish ourselves, but to im- 
ss on our minds the determination of avoiding it in the 
cure. 
All that has been said applies especially to blasphemy 
|d perjury. Beware also of that vice known as cursing. The 
me at whose mention “every knee in heaven, on earth, 
id in hell should bow down’”’ in reverence (Phil. 2:10) 
ould be used only with devotion and affection. Strive, 
refore, to speak with piety of the holy Name of God, and 
» what you can by your prayers, your exhortations, and 
‘ur example to banish the terrible evil of which we have 
ren speaking. 


304 The Sinner’s Guide 


SECTION II 
On Detraction and Raillery 


The abominable sin of detraction is so prevalent at the 
present day that there is scarcely a society, a family, an in: 
dividual not guilty of it. There are some persons s¢ 
perversely inclined that they cannot bear to hear any gooc 
of another, but are always alive to their neighbor’s faults 
always ready to tear his character to pieces. 

To excite in your heart a salutary hatred of this detesta 
ble and dangerous vice, consider the three great evils whick 
it involves. First, it always borders upon mortal sin, ever 
when it is not actually such. From criticisms and censures 
with which people generally begin, we easily fall int 
detraction or calumny. Detraction is committed when we 
tell another’s real faults; calumny, when the fault we men 
tion is not real, but the invention of our malicious lies 
Thus, though we may not be guilty of calumny, how oftet 
does it happen that a person, from criticizing the failings o 
others which are generally known, is gradually led to men 
tion some hidden and grave sin which robs him of hi 
reputation and his honor! That the fault revealed is true it 
no manner saves the detractor from the guilt of mortal sin 

The descent to such a crime is easy; for when the tongu 
of the detractor is started, and a desire to embellish his stor 
seizes him, it is as difficult to restrain him as to extinguish | 
fire fanned by a high wind, or to stop a horse when he ha 
taken the bit in his teeth and is dashing madly on. It is th 
fear of this evil which led the author of Ecclesiasticus to er 
out: “Who will set a guard before my mouth and a sure sez 
upon my lips, that I fall not by them, and that my tongu 
destroy me not?” (Ecclus. 22:33). He keenly realized th 
difficulties in the way, knowing, as Solomon says, that “‘it i 
the part of man to prepare the soul, and of the Lord t 
govern the tongue.” (Prov. 16:1). 


Other Sins to be avoided 305 


The second evil of this vice consists in the threefold in- 

wy which it inflicts—namely, on the one who speaks, on 
-m who listens with approval, and on the victim who is 
lssailed in his absence. 
In addition to this, the person who complacently listens 
» detraction is frequently a talebearer. To ingratiate him- 
if with the victims of the detraction he carries to them all 
at has been said against them, and thus excites enmities 
hich are seldom extinguished, and which sometimes end 
ven in bloodshed. ‘‘The whisperer and the double-tongued 
3s accursed,” we are told in the Sacred Scriptures, “for he 
ath troubled many that were at peace.” (Ecclus. 28:15). 

To teach us the baneful effects of this insidious vice, the 
Holy Ghost compares it at one time to the swift blow of a 
sharp razor” (Ps. 51:4); at another time to the bite of the 
oisonous asp, (Cf. Ps. 13:3), which disappears, but leaves 
its venom in the wound. With reason, then, did the author of 
Scclesiasticus say: “The stroke of a whip maketh a blue 

ark, but the stroke of the tongue will break the bones.” 
|Ecclus. 28:21). 
| The third evil of this vice is the horror it inspires and the 
infamy which it brings upon us. Men fly from a detractor as 
uaturally as they would from a venomous serpent. “A man 
ke of tongue,” says Holy Scripture, “‘is terrible in his city, 
nd he that is rash in his word shall be hateful.’ (Ecclus. 
:25). Are not these evils sufficient to make you abhor a 
ice so injurious and so unprofitable? Why will you make 
rourself odious in the sight of God and men for a sin from 
which you can reap no advantage? Remember, moreover, 
hat in no other vice do we so quickly form a habit, for ev- 
rry time we speak with others we expose ourselves to the 
langer of relapsing. 
 Henceforward consider your neighbor’s character as a 
orbidden tree which you cannot touch. Be no less slow in 
yraising yourself than in censuring others, for the first indi- 


306 The Sinner’s Guide 


cates vanity and the second a want of charity. Speak of the 
virtues of your neighbor, but be silent as to his faults. Let 
nothing that you say lead others to think that he is aught but 
a man of virtue and honor. You will thus avoid innumerable 
sins and much remorse of conscience; you will be pleasing 
to God and men; and you will be respected by all as you 
respect others. Put a bridle upon your tongue and learn to 
withhold an angry word when your heart is moved. Believe 
me, there is no control more difficult and at the same time 
more noble and advantageous than that which a wise man 
exercises over his tongue. Do not think yourself guiltless 
because you artfully mingle your malicious insinuations 
with words of praise. In this respect the detractor is like the 
surgeon, who soothingly passes his hand over the vein 
before piercing it with the lancet: “His words are smoother 
than oil, and the same are darts.” (Ps. 54:22). 

To refrain from speaking ill of others is always a virtue, 
but it is a still greater virtue to refrain from reviling those 
who have injured us; for the greater the injured feeling 
which prompts us to speak, the greater is our generosity in 
resisting it. 

Nor is it sufficient not to indulge in detraction; you must 
also endeavor to avoid hearing it. Be faithful to the counsel 
of the Holy Spirit, who tells you to “hedge in thy ears with 
thorns, and hear not a wicked tongue.” (Ecclus. 28:28). Ob- 
serve that you are not told to hedge in your ears with cotton, 
but with thorns, that you may not only repel the words of 
the detractor, but that you may pierce him, and, by showing 
him a grave countenance, teach him how displeasing to you 
is his conduct. 

“The north wind driveth away rain,” says Solomon, “as 
doth a sad countenance a backbiting tongue.” (Prov. 
25:23). Impose silence, therefore, upon the detractor, if he 
be your inferior or one whom you can reprove without 
offense. If you cannot do this, prudently endeavor to turn 


Other Sins to be avoided 307 


© conversation, or show by the severity of your counte- 
sce that his conversation is not pleasing to you. Beware 
nearing the detractor with smiling attention, for you thus 
courage him, and consequently share in his guilt. It is a 
jevous offense to set fire to a house, but it is scarcely less 
ilpable to stand idly by witnessing its destruction instead 
aiding in extinguishing the flames. 

But of all detractions, that which is directed against vir- 
,ous persons is the most sinful. It not only injures the per- 
bn assailed, but tends to discourage others who are begin- 
2rs in virtue, while it confirms the cowardice of those who 
‘ill not risk our censures by striving to do good. For what 
ould be no scandal or stumbling block to the strong may 
ove an insurmountable obstacle to the weak. If you would 
ppreciate the evil of this kind of scandal, reflect upon these 
rords of Our Saviour: “He that shall scandalize one of 
nese little ones that believe in me, it were better for him 
nat a millstone should be hanged about his neck, and that 
‘¢ should be drowned in the depth of the sea.” (Matt. 18:6). 
avoid, therefore, as you would a sacrilege, all scandalous 
eflections upon persons consecrated to God. If their con- 
juct furnish matter for censure, nevertheless continue to 
spect the sacred character with which they are invested, 
pr it is of them that Our Saviour has said: “He that 
pucheth you, toucheth the apple of my eye.” (Zach. 2:8). 
| All that we have said of detraction applies with still more 
ie to those who make others the object of derision and 
aillery; for this vice, besides having all the evil conse- 
juences of the first two, presupposes pride, presumption, 
nd contempt for one’s neighbor. In the Old Law God 
pecially warns us against it: “Thou shalt not be a detrac- 
or, nor a whisperer among the people.” (Lev. 19:16). We 
\ave no need to insist upon the enormity of this vice; what 
ve have said on the subject of detraction is sufficient. 


308 The Sinner’s Guide 


SECTION III 
@ On Rash Judgments 


Those who are addicted to detraction and raillery do not 
confine themselves to what they know, but indulge in sup: 
positions and rash judgments. When they no longer find 
matter to censure they invent evil intentions, misinterpret 
good actions, forgetting that Our Saviour has said: “Judge 
not, that you may not be judged; for with what judgment 
you judge you shall be judged.” (Matt. 7:1-2). Here also the 
offense may frequently be a mortal sin, particularly when 
we venture to judge in a matter of grave importance upon 
very slight evidence. If it be only a suspicion, not a real 
judgment, it may be only a venial sin, because the act has 
not been completed. Even by suspicion, however, a mortal 


sin can be committed by suspecting virtuous persons of 
enormous crimes. 


SECTION IV 
On the Commandments of the Church 


Besides these sins against the Commandments of God 
there are those against the commandments of the Church, 
which also impose upon us a grave obligation. Such are the 
precepts to hear Mass on Sundays and holy days of obliga- 
tion; to confess our sins at least once a year, and to receive 
the Holy Eucharist at Easter or thereabouts: to pay tithes to 
our pastor, and to observe the days of fasting and absti- 
nence prescribed by the Church. The precept of fasting is 
binding from the age of 21 and upwards; that of abstinence 
obliges all who have attained the age of reason. The sick, 
the convalescent, nursing women, women in pregnancy, 
those whose labors are severe, and those who are too poor 
to afford one full meal a day, are exempt from the law of 
fasting. There may be other lawful reasons for dispensation, 


Other Sins to be avoided 309 


« which the faithful ought to apply to their pastor or con- 
ssor, and not take it upon themselves to set aside the law 
the Church. 
The difference between abstinence and fasting should be 
membered. By fasting we mean eating only one full meal 
the day, with a slight collation in the evening. By absti- 
bnce we mean giving up the use of flesh-meat. It should be 
>rne in mind, therefore, on Ember days and at other times 
* fast, that the law is not fulfilled by simply abstaining 
om meat. Unless you are excused by some of the reasons 
ven above or by dispensation, you must observe the fast 
y eating only one full meal, with the collation in the eve- 
ing, and a warm drink, with a cracker or small piece of 
read, in the morning. 
_In regard to hearing Mass, we must endeavor to be pres- 
t at the Holy Sacrifice not only in body but in mind, with 
be and recollection, having our thoughts fixed upon the 
uystery of the altar, or upon some other pious subject. The 
scital of devout prayers, especially the Rosary, is an ex- 
ellent means of keeping ourselves united with God. If we 
re at the head of a house we must be careful to see that all 
inder our charge hear Mass, not only on Sundays, but also 
n holy days. Too much laxity regarding holy days is apt to 
revail among those who earn their bread by the sweat of 
seir brow. They should remember that the obligation to 
ear Mass on a holy day is the same as the obligation to 
ear it on Sunday. Consequently, they must make serious 
be sincere efforts to comply with this duty. To attend an 
arly Mass may involve the loss of a little sleep, but they 
nould remember that these holy days occur but seldom, 
nd that they must do something to atone for their sins and 
merit the kingdom of Heaven. Parents and employers will 
ave a severe account to render to God if they cause or per- 
1it those confided to their care to neglect this sacred duty. 
VJhen there is a just reason, such as the care of the sick or 


310 The Sinner’s Guide 


any other pressing necessity which prevents our hearin 
Mass, we are released from the obligation. 


CHAPTER 38 


Venial Sins 


hough the sins of which we have been treating are 
se which we should avoid with most care, yet do not 
ink that you are dispensed from vigilance in regard to 
nial sins. I conjure you not to be one of those 
ais Christians who make no scruple of committing 
. because it is venial. Remember these words of Holy 
ripture: “He that contemneth small things shall fall by 
le and little.” (Ecclus. 19:1). “Do not despise venial 
is because they appear trifling,” says St. Augustine, “but 
ur them because they are numerous. Small animals in 
ige numbers can kill a man. Grains of sand are very 
jall, yet, if accumulated, they can sink a ship. Drops of 
iter are very small, yet how often they become a mighty 
er, a raging torrent, sweeping everything before them!”’ 
he holy Doctor goes on to observe that though no 
mber of venial sins can constitute a mortal sin, yet these 
hter failings predispose us to greater faults, which 
len become mortal. St. Gregory observes with equal 
ith that slight faults are sometimes more dangerous than 
eater ones, for the latter, when we behold their hideous- 
ss, awaken remorse and resolutions of amendment, but 
> former make less impression on us, and thus, by easily 
apsing into them, we soon contract a strong habit. 
Finally, venial sin, however slight, is always prejudicial 
the soul. It weakens our devotion, troubles the peace of 


311 


312 The Sinner’s Guide 


our conscience, diminishes the fervor of charity, exhaus 
the strength of our spiritual life, and obstructs the work « 
the Holy Ghost in our souls. I pray you then to do all 

your power to avoid these sins, for there is no enemy tc 
weak to harm us if we make no resistance. Slight ange 
gluttony, vanity, idle words and thoughts, immodera 
laughter, loss of time, too much sleeping, trivial lies « 
flatteries—such are the sins against which I would pa 
ticularly warn you. Great vigilance is required again 
offenses of this kind, for occasions of venial sin abound.

pleasure in the choice of his food. He has perfectly 
triumphed over this vice who not only limits the quantity of 
his food, but who denies himself delicacies except when 
necessity, charity, or politeness prompts him to accept 
them.

CHAPTER 40 


The Three Kinds of Virtues in which the 
Fullness of Justice Consists; and first, Man’s 
Duty to Himself 


SECTION I 
Our Threefold Obligation to Virtue 


Having spoken at length of the sins which profane and 
degrade the soul, let us now turn to the virtues which ele- 
vate and adorn it with the spiritual treasures of justice. It 
belongs to justice to render to everyone his due: to God, to 
our neighbor, and to ourselves. If we faithfully acquit our- 
selves of these duties to God, to our neighbor, and to our- 
selves, we fulfill the obligations of justice and thus become 
truly virtuous. 

To accomplish this great work let your heart be that of a 
son towards God, that of a brother towards your neighbor, 
and that of a judge towards yourself. In this, the prophet 
tells us, the virtue of man consists: “I will show thee, O 
man, what is good and what the Lord requireth of thee: 
Verily, to do judgment, and to love mercy, and to walk 
solicitous with thy God.” (Mich. 6:8). The duty of judgment 
is what man owes to himself; the duty of mercy what he 
owes to his neighbor; and to walk carefully before God is 
the duty he owes to his Creator. 


320 


Man’s Duty to Himself 321) 


SECTION II 
The Reformation of the Body 


Charity, it is truly said, begins at home. Let us, therefore, 
Segin with the first obligation mentioned by the prophet— 
he duty of judgment which man must exercise towards 
mimself. Every just judge must enforce order and discipline 
in the district over which he exercises jurisdiction. Now, the 
kingdom over which man rules is divided into two distinct 
arts: the body with all its organs and senses, and the soul 
with all its affections and powers. Over all these he must es- 
ablish the empire of virtue, if he would faithfully perform 
is duty to himself. 
| To reform the body and bring it under the dominion of 
virtue, the first thing to be acquired is a modest and 
idecorous bearing. “Let there be nothing in your carriage, 
our deportment, or your dress,” says St. Augustine, “capa- 
ble of scandalizing your neighbor, but let everything about 
you be conformable to the purity and sanctity of your 
rofession.” Hence a servant of God should bear himself 
: ith gravity, humility, and sweetness, that all who ap- 
roach him may profit by his example and be edified by his 
virtues. The great Apostle would have us, like fragrant 
plants, giving forth the sweet perfume of piety and filling all 
about us with the odor of Jesus Christ. (Cf. 2 Cor. 2:15). 
| Such, indeed, should be the effect of the words, the ac- 
tions, and the bearing of those who serve God, so that none 
who draw near to them can resist the sweet attraction of 
sanctity. This is one of the principal fruits of a modest and 
recollected deportment. It is a mute but eloquent teaching, 
which draws men to the love of virtue and the service of 
God. Thus do we fulfill the precept of Our Saviour: “So let 
your light shine before men that they may see your good 
works, and glorify your Father who is in heaven.” (Matt. 
5:16). The prophet Isaias also tells us that God’s servants 


B22 The Sinner’s Guide 


should be plants bearing fruits of righteousness and virtue, 
the beauty of which will lead men to extol the power of their _ 
Creator. (Cf. Js. 61:3). This does not mean that our good | 
works must be done to gain the applause of men, for, as St. 
Gregory tells us, “a good work may be public only while its 
intention remains a secret between God and the soul. The — 
example we thus afford our brethren destroys neither the 
merit of humility nor the desire to please only God.” | 
(Moral. 29,18). 

Another fruit which we derive from this exterior modesty — 
is a greater facility in preserving the recollection, devotion, 
and purity of the soul. The interior and the exterior man are 
so closely united that good or evil in one is quickly com- 
municated to the other. If order reign in the soul, its effect is 
experienced in the body; and the body, if disturbed, renders 
the soul likewise restless. Each may in all respects be con- 
sidered a mirror of the other, for the actions of one are 
faithfully represented in the other. For this reason a com- 
posed and modest bearing must contribute to interior 
recollection and modesty, while a restless exterior must be 
incompatible with peace of soul. Hence the Wise Man tells 
us: “He that is hasty with his feet shall stumble.” (Prov. 
19:2). Thus would he teach us that he whose exterior is 
wanting in that calm gravity which is the distinctive mark of 
God’s servants must inevitably stumble and frequently fall. 

A third effect of the virtue we are considering is to com- 
municate to man a composure and gravity befitting any of- 
fice he may fill. We behold an example of this in Job, who 
tells us that the light (the dignity) of his countenance never 
fell to the earth. (Cf. Job 29:24). And speaking of the 
authority of his bearing, he says: “The young men saw me 
and hid themselves, and the old men rose up and stood. The 
princes ceased to speak, and laid the finger on their mouth. 
The rulers held their peace, and their tongue cleaved to 
their throat.”’ (Job 29:8-10). But the gravity and dignity of 


Man’s Duty to Himself Bes 


this holy man were mingled with so much sweetness and 
nercy that, as he tells us, when seated as a king with his 
army about him he was a comforter to them that mourned. 
KCf. Job 25). 

Wise men condemn this want of modest gravity less as a 
fault in itself than as a mark of levity; for, as we have 
already observed, an unreserved and frivolous exterior in- 
dicates an uncontrolled and ill-regulated interior. Hence 
he author of Ecclesiasticus says: “The attire of the body, 
nd the laughter of the teeth, and the gait of the man show 
what he is.” (Ecclus. 19:27). “As the faces of them that look 
herein shine in the water,” says Solomon, “‘so the hearts of 
en are laid open to the wise” by their exterior acts. (Prov. 
27:19). 

Such are the benefits which result from a grave and 

odest deportment. We cannot but deplore the conduct of 
had who, through human respect, laugh and jest with a 
freedom unbecoming their profession, and allow them- 
selves indulgences which deprive them of many of the fruits 
of virtue. “A religious,” says St. John Climachus, “should 
mot abandon his fasts through fear of falling into the sin of 
vainglory.” Neither should fear of the world’s displeasure 

ause us to lose the advantages of gravity and modesty in 
hs conduct; for it is as unreasonable to sacrifice a virtue 
through fear of offending men as it would be to seek to 
overcome one vice by another. 
ee preceding remarks apply to our manners in general. 
We shall next treat of the modesty and sobriety which we 
should observe at table. 


| SECTION III 
Temperance 
| 


The first thing to be done for the reformation of the body 
is to put a rigorous curb on the appetites and to refrain from 


324 The Sinner’s Guide 


immoderate indulgence of any of the senses. As myrrh, 
which is an exceedingly bitter substance, preserves the 
body from corruption after death, so mortification 
preserves it during life from the corruption of vice. For this 
reason we shall consider the efficacy of sobriety, or tem- 
perance—a virtue upon which all the others depend, but 
which is very difficult to attain because of the resistance of 
our corrupt nature. | 

Read, then, the words in which the Holy Spirit deigns to 
instruct us in this respect: “Use as.a frugal man the things 
that are set before thee, lest if thou eatest much thou be 
hated. Leave off first for manners’ sake, and exceed not lest 
thou offend. And if thou sittest among many, reach not thy 
hand out first of all, and be not the first to ask for drink.” 
(Ecclus. 31:19-21). Here are rules worthy of the Sovereign 
Master, who wills that we should imitate in our actions the 
decorum and order which reign in all His works. St. Ber- 
nard teaches us the same lesson in these words: “In regard 
to eating there are four things to be regulated: the time, the 
manner, the quantity, and the quality. The time should be 
limited to the usual hours of our repast; the manner should 
be free from that eagerness which makes us appear ab- 
sorbed in what is set before us; the quantity and quality 
should not exceed what is granted others, except when a 
condition of health manifestly requires delicacies.” (Ep. ad 
Fratres de Monte Dei.). 

In forcible words, supported by appropriate examples, 
St. Gregory declares the same sentiments: “It belongs to 
abstinence not to anticipate the ordinary time of meals, as 
Jonathan did when he ate the honeycomb (Cf. / Kg. 14:27); 
not to desire the greatest delicacies, as the Israelites did in 
the desert when they longed for the fleshpots of Egypt (Cf. 
Exod. 16:3); not to wish for the choicest preparation of 
food, as the people of Sodom (Cf. Gen. 19); and not to yield 
to greediness, as Esau did (Cf. Gen. 25:33) when he sold his 


Man’s Duty to Himself 525 


purthright for a mess of pottage.” (Moral. 30,27). 

Hugh of St. Victor tells us we must be very attentive to 
«ur deportment at table, always observing a certain 
modesty of the eyes and a reserve of speech. There are 
ome, he says, who are no sooner seated at table than their 
controlled appetite is manifested by their bearing: Their 
-yes eagerly scan the whole board; they rudely help them- 
selves before others, and seize upon the nearest dish, 
egardless of all save self. They approach the table as a 
zeneral approaches a fort which he is to assail, as if they 
lvere considering how they can most quickly consume all 
hat lies before them. (Discip. Monast.). Control these dis- 
raceful indications of a degrading vice, and overcome the 
vice itself by restricting the quantity and quality of your 
rood. Bear these wise counsels in mind at all times, but par- 
licularly when the appetite is stimulated by hunger, or by 
are and sumptuous viands which prove strong incentives to 
luttony. 

Beware of the illusions of this vice, which St. John 
Climachus tells us is most deceptive. At the beginning of a 
repast it is so clamorous that it would seem that no amount 
ould satisfy our hunger; but if we are firm in resisting its 
nruly demands, we shall see that a moderate portion is 
ufficient for nature. 

An excellent remedy against gluttony is to bear in mind 
hen we go to table that there are, as a pagan philosopher 
says, two guests to be provided for: the body, to which we 
ust furnish the food which its necessity craves; and our 
soul, which we must maintain by the virtues of self-denial 
nd temperance. A no less efficacious remedy is to compare 
the happy fruits of abstinence with the gross pleasures of 
zluttony, which will enable us to appreciate the folly of 
sacrificing such lasting advantages for such pernicious and 
leeting gratifications. 

Remember, moreover, that of all the pleasures of the 


326 The Sinner’s Guide 


senses those of taste and feeling are the lowest. We have 
them in common with all animals, even the most imperfect, 
while there are many which lack the other three, seeing, 
hearing, and smelling. These former senses, tasting and 
feeling, are not only the basest, but their pleasures are the 
least enduring, for they vanish with the object which pro- 
duced them. 

Add to these considerations the thought of the sufferings 
of the martyrs, and the fasts and mortifications of the saints. 
Think, too, of your many sins which must be expiated; of 
the pains of Purgatory; of the torments of Hell. Each of 
these things will tell you how necessary it is to take up the 
cross, to overcome your appetites, and to do penance for the 
sinful gratifications of the past. Remember, then, the duty 
of self-denial; prepare for your necessary meals with such 
reflections before your mind, and you will see how easy it 
will be to observe the rules of moderation and sobriety. 

Though this great prudence is necessary in eating, how 
much more is required in drinking! There is nothing more 
injurious to chastity than the excessive use of wine, in 
which, as the Apostle says, there is luxury. (Cf. Eph. 5:18). 
It is at all times the capital enemy of this angelic virtue; but 
it is particularly in youth that such indulgence is most fatal. 
Hence St. Jerome says that wine and youth are two incen- 
tives to impurity. (Ad Eustoch, de Cust. Virg.). Wine is to 
youth what fuel is to fire. As oil poured upon the flames 
only increases their intensity, so wine, like a violent con- 
flagration, heats the blood, enkindling and exciting the pas- 
sions to the highest pitch of folly and madness. Witness the 
excesses into which man is led by hatred, love, revenge, and 
other passions, when stimulated by intoxicating liquors. 
The natural effect of this fatal indulgence is to counteract 
all the results of the moral virtues. These subdue and con- 
trol the baser passions, but wine excites and urges them to 
the wildest licentiousness. Judge, therefore, with what 


Man’s Duty to Himself 27 


vigilance you should guard against the attacks of such an 
enemy. 
Remember, too, that by wine is meant every kind of drink 
capable of robbing man of the use of his reason or his 
senses. A philosopher has wisely said that the vine bears 
hree kinds of grapes: one for necessity, one-for pleasure, 
and one for folly. In other words, wine taken with modera- 
ion supports our weakness; beyond this limit it only flatters 
he senses; and drunk to excess it produces a species of 
adness. Heed no inspiration or thought which you have 
eason to think is excited by wine, the worst of evil coun- 
selors. 
| Avoid with equal care all disputes or arguments at table, 
for they are often the beginning of grave quarrels. Be no 
lless moderate in speech than in the indulgence of your ap- 
petite; for, as Holy Scripture tells us, “there is no secret 
where drunkenness reigneth.” (Prov. 31:4). We shall find 
rather unbridled tongues, immoderate laughter, vulgar 
jokes, violent disputes, the revelation of secrets, and many 
jother unhappy consequences of intemperance. 
_ Another evil against which I would warn you is dwelling 
pon the merits of certain dishes, and condemning others 
ecause they are not so delicate. How unworthy it is of man 
ito fix his mind and heart on eating and drinking with such 
eagerness that the burden of his conversation is on the ex- 
cellent fish of such a river, the luscious fruit of such a coun- 
try, and the fine wines of such a region! This is a clear proof 
that he has lost sight of the true end of eating, which is to 
support nature, and that, instead of devoting to this work 
the senses destined for it, he debases his heart and his in- 
telligence to make them also slaves of his gluttony. 
Avoid with especial care all attacks upon your neighbor’s 
character. The malicious rapacity which prompts us to tear 
our neighbor’s reputation in pieces was justly condemned 
by St. John Chrysostom as a species of cannibalism: “Will 


328 The Sinner’s Guide 


you not be satisfied with eating the flesh of animals? Must 
you devour human flesh by robbing another of his good 
name?” St. Augustine had so great a horror for this vice, 
from which so few tables are free, that he inscribed on the 
walls of his dining room the following lines: 


“This board allows no vile detractor place 
Whose tongue will charge the absent with disgrace.” 
—Vita Aug; c. 22 


Still another point to which I wish to direct your attention 
is the warning given by St. Jerome, that it is better to eat 
moderately every day than to fast for several days and then 
to eat to excess. A gentle rain, he says, in proper season 
benefits the earth, but violent floods only devastate it. (Ep. 
7 ad Loec.). 

Finally, let necessity, not pleasure, govern you in eating 
and drinking. I do not say that you must allow your body to 
want for nourishment. Oh, no; like any animal destined for 
the service of man, your body must be supported. All that is 
required is to control it, and never to eat solely for pleasure. 
We must conquer, not destroy, the flesh, says St. Bernard; 
we must keep it in subjection, that it may not grow proud, 
for it belongs to it to obey, not to govern. (Ep. ad FF, de 
Monte Dei.). 

This will suffice to show the importance of this virtue. 
But he who would learn more of the happy fruits of tem- 
perance, and its salutary effects not only upon the soul but 
even upon health, life, honor, and happiness, may read a 
special treatise on this subject which we have added to our 
book on meditation and prayer.

SECTION IV 
The Government of the Senses 


The next step in the reformation of the body is the 
government of the senses. These are the avenues which a 
Christian should guard with special care, particularly the 
eyes, which, in the language of Holy Scripture, are the win- 
dows through which death enters to rob us of life. Persons 
desirous of making progress in prayer should be very 
vigilant in guarding this sense, for this watchfulness not 
only promotes recollection, but is a most efficacious means 
of preserving chastity. Without this guard they are a prey to 
all the vanities which surround them, and which take such 
/ possession of the imagination that it is impossible to banish 
\them during prayer. This is the reason of the modesty of the 
eyes which devout souls observe. Not only do they avoid 
images which could tarnish the purity of their hearts, but 
ithey resolutely turn their eyes from curious objects and 
‘worldly vanities, that their mind and heart may be free to 
iconverse with God without distraction, and to advance in 
the knowledge of spiritual things. Prayer is so delicate an 
iexercise that it is impeded not only by sinful images, but 
jalso by the representation of objects otherwise harmless in 
themselves. 

The sense of hearing requires a no less vigilant guard, for 
through it we learn a multitude of things which weary, dis- 
tract, and even defile the soul. We should protect our ears 
not only from evil words, but from frivolous conversations, 
worldly gossip, and idle discourses. During meditation we 
suffer from a want of vigilance in this respect, for these 
ines are great obstacles to recollection, and persistently 
interpose between God and the soul in time of prayer. 

Little need be said of the sense of smell, for an inordinate 
love of perfumes and sweet essences is so sensual and so 
effeminate that most men are ashamed of it, for this is a 


| 


330 The Sinner’s Guide 


gratification in which few but women indulge. 


SECTION V 
The Government of the Tongue 


Here is a subject upon which there is much to be said, for _ 
we are told in Holy Scripture that ‘‘death and life are in the 
power of the tongue.”’ (Prov. 18:21). From this we can — 
understand that the happiness or misery of every man de- 
pends upon the use he makes of this organ. 

St. James asserts this truth no less strongly when he says, — 
“If any man offend not in word, the same is a perfect man. 
He is able also with a bridle to lead about the whole body. 
We put bits into the mouths of horses that they may obey us, 
and we turn about their whole body. Behold also ships, 
whereas they are great and are driven by strong winds, yet 
are they turned about with a small helm whithersoever the 
force of the governor willeth. So the tongue also is, indeed, 
a little member and boasteth great things. Behold how small 
a fire kindleth a great wood. And the tongue is a fire, a 
world of iniquity.” (James 3:2-6). To govern this great in- 
strument for good we must bear in mind, when we speak, 
four things: of what we speak, how we speak, the time we 
speak, and the object for which we speak. 

In regard to the first point, what we speak, remember the 
counsel of the Apostle: “Let no evil speech proceed from 
your mouth, but that which is good to the edification of 
faith, that it may administer grace to the hearers. All 
uncleanness, or covetousness, let it not be so much as 
named among you, as becometh saints, or obscenity, or 
foolish talking, or scurrility.” (Eph. 4:29 and 5:3-4). As the 
sailor always bears with him a chart indicating the shoals 
and rocks which could wreck his vessel, so should the 
Christian bear with him these counsels of the Apostle in- 
dicating the shoals of speech which could wreck him in his 


Man’s Duty to Himself 331 


/oyage to eternity. Be no less careful in guarding a secret 
vhich has been confided to you, for the betrayal of a trust is 
me of the vilest faults into which the tongue can lead us. 

In regard to the second point, how we are to speak, let us 
sbserve a just medium between silence and talkativeness, 
setween timidity and self-sufficiency, between frivolity and 
omposity; always speaking with becoming gravity, 
noderation, sweetness, and simplicity. Beware of haughtily 
asserting and obstinately persisting in your statements, for 
his fault gives rise to disputes which wound charity and 
estroy the peace of the soul. It is the part of a generous 
ature to yield in such contentions, and a prudent man will 
ollow the counsel of the inspired writer: “In many things 
e as if thou wert ignorant, and hear in silence and withal 
beeking.” (Ecclus. 32:12). 

Consider also the necessity of observing when you speak, 
and always endeavor to select a suitable time: “A parable 
roming out of a fool’s mouth shall be rejected, for he doth 
not speak it in due season.” (Ecclus. 20:22). 

_ Finally, we must consider the end for which we speak. 
There are some whose only purpose is to appear learned. 
Dthers desire to parade their wit and conversational 
powers. The first are thus led into hypocrisy and deceit, and 
ne second become the sport of self-love and vanity. It does 
not suffice, therefore, that our conversation be good in it- 
,elf—it must be directed to some good end, such as the glo- 
of God or the profit of our neighbor. 

In addition to this we must also consider the persons to 
whom we speak. For example, it does not become the 
young to engross the conversation in the presence of their 
riders, nor the ignorant in the presence of the learned, nor 
ay persons in the presence of ecclesiastics or religious. 
When you have reason to think that your words may be un- 
imely or presumptuous, be silent. All persons are not capa- 
le of judging correctly in these points, and therefore, in 


B32 The Sinner’s Guide 


doubt, the wisest course is a prudent silence. We shall thus 
conform to all the rules we have been considering; for, as 
the Wise Man says, “Even a fool, if he will hold his peace, 
shall be counted wise; and if he close his lips, a man of 
understanding.” (Prov. 17:28). 


SECTION VI 
The Mortification of the Passions 


Having thus regulated the body and all its senses, the 
most important reformation still remains to be effected, 
which is that of the soul with all its powers. Here the first to 
present itself is the sensitive appetite which comprises all 
our natural affections: love, hatred, joy, sorrow, fear, hope, 
anger, and other sentiments of a like nature. This appetite is 
the inferior part of the soul, which gives us our strongest 
resemblance to irrational animals, because, like them, it is 
guided solely by inclination. Nothing degrades us more or 
leads us further from God. Hence St. Bernard says that if 
we take away self-love, by which he understands all the 
movements of the sensitive appetite, there will be no longer 
any reason for the existence of Hell. (De Resurrectione 
Dni., Serm. 3). 

The sensitive appetite is the arsenal which supplies sin 
with its most dangerous arms. It is the vulnerable part of the 
soul, a second Eve, frail and inconstant, heeding the wiles 
of the old serpent and dragging with her in her fall the 
unhappy Adam—that is, the superior part of the soul, the 
seat of the will and the understanding. Original sin is here 
manifested in all its power. Here the malignity of its poison 
is concentrated. Here is the field of man’s combats, defeats 
and victories. Here is the school in which virtue is exercise¢ 
and trained, for all our courage, all our merit consists ir 
overcoming the blind passions which spring from the sensi. 
tive appetite. 


Man’s Duty to Himself 333 


This is why our soul is represented sometimes as a vine 
needing the careful pruning of the husbandman; sometimes 
#8 a garden from which the gardener must diligently uproot 
ke weeds of vice to give place to the plants of virtues. It 
should be the principal occupation of our lives, therefore, 
io cultivate this garden, ruthlessly plucking from our soul 
all that can choke the growth of good. We shall thus become 
‘rue children of God, guided by the motions of the Holy 
Ghost. We shall thus live as spiritual men, following the 
Zuidance of grace and the dictates of reason, and not as 
‘hose carnal men who, following the irrational animals, 
obey only the impulse of passion. This subjection of the 
ensitive appetite is the mortification so much commended 
: Scripture; the death to which the Apostle so frequently 
xhorts us; the practice of justice and truth so constantly ex - 
|olled by David and the other prophets. Therefore, let it be 
the object of all our labors, all our prayers, and all our 
pious exercises. 
_ Each one should carefully study his own disposition and 
inclinations, in order to place the most vigilant guard on the 
weakest side of his nature. We must wage constant war 
sgainst all our appetites, but it is particularly necessary to 
-ombat the desire of honors, of riches, and of pleasures, for 
hese are the roots of all evil. 

Beware, too, of that pride which bears with no opposi- 
ion. It is a fault which prevails among persons of elevated 
tation accustomed to command, and to deny themselves no 
raprice. To conquer it, learn to deny yourself innocent grat- 

ications, that you may more easily sacrifice those which 
re unlawful. Learn to bear contradictions with a dignity 
ind patience worthy of a creature who was not made for the 
hings of this world, but who aspires to immortality. Such 
xercises will render us skillful in the use of spiritual weap- 
ns, which require no less practice than is necessary for the 
/roper management of material arms. Much more impor- 


334 The Sinner’s Guide 


tant, however, is a skillful use of the former, for a victory 
over self, over pride, or over any passion far outweighs all 
the conquests of the world. Humble yourself, then, in the 
performance of lowly and obscure works, regardless of the 
world’s opinion; for what can it take from us, or what can it 
give us, when our inheritance is God Himself? 


SECTION VII 
The Reformation of the Will 


One of the most efficacious means of effecting this refor- 
mation is to strengthen and adorn the superior will—that is, 
the rational appetite—with humility of heart, poverty of 
spirit, and a holy hatred of self. If we possess these, the 
labor of mortification is easily accomplished. Humility, ac- 
cording to the definition of St. Bernard, is contempt of self 
founded on a true knowledge of our baseness. The effect of 
this virtue is to pluck from our heart all the roots of pride as 
well as all love of earthly honors and dignities. It inspires us 
to seek the lowest place, persuading us that had another 
received the graces we enjoy he would have been more 
grateful and would have used them more profitably for the 
glory of God. It is not sufficient that man cherish these sen- 
timents in his heart; they should also be evident in his 
deportment and surroundings, which, regardless of the 
world’s opinion, should be as humble and simple as his 
position will admit. And while he maintains the dignity due 
to his station his heart should ever be ready to submit no 
only to superiors and equals, but even to inferiors for the 
love of God. 

The second disposition required to strengthen and adort 
the will is poverty of spirit, which consists in a voluntary 
contempt for the things of this world, and in a perfect con 
tentment in the position in which God has placed us 
however poor and lowly it may be. This virtue effectualh 


Man’s Duty to Himself 335 


siroys cupidity, and affords us so great a peace and con- 
fiment that Seneca did not hesitate to affirm that he who 
tsed his heart to the claims of unruly desires was not in- 
ior in wealth or happiness to Jupiter himself. By this he 
-nified that as man’s misery springs from unfulfilled 
sires, he may be said to be very near the summit of happi- 
3s who has learned to subdue his desires so that they can- 
é disturb him. 
The third disposition is a holy hatred of ourselves. “He 
at loveth his life shall lose it,” says Our Saviour, ‘“‘and he 
it hateth his life in this world keepeth it unto life eternal.” 
1, 12:25). By this hatred of self Our Lord did not mean 
tt wicked hatred in which they indulge who yield to 
ispair, but that aversion which the saints experienced for 
it flesh, which they regarded as the source of many evils 
as a great obstacle to good. Hence they subjected it to 
2 empire of reason, and denied its inordinate desires, that 
Light continue a humble servant and willing helper of the 
ul. 
If we treat it otherwise we shall realize these words of the 
ise Man: “He that nourisheth his servant delicately from 
3 childhood, afterwards shall find him stubborn.” (Prov. 
in This hatred of self is our chief instrument in the 
rk of salvation. It enables us to uproot and cast from us 
our evil inclinations, however much nature may rebel. 
ithout it how could we strike rude blows, penetrate to the 
ick with the knife of mortification, and tear from our 
rts objects upon which our affections are centered? Yes, 
2 arm of mortification, which draws its force as much 
/m hatred of self as from love of God, enables us to treat 
rt failings with the firmness of a skillful physician, and 
entlessly to cut and burn with no other thought than to 
| the soul of every evil tendency. Having developed this 
Ree in the Memorial of a Christian Life, we shall not 
re speak of it at greater length.

Besides these two faculties of the sensitive appetite ther 
are two others, imagination and understanding, whic. 
belong to the intellect. The imagination, a less elevate 
power than the understanding, is of all the faculties the on 
in which the effects of original sin are most evident, an 
which is least under the control of reason. It continuall 
escapes our vigilance, and like a restless child runs hithe 
and thither, sometimes flying to the remotest corners of th 
world before we are aware of its ramblings. It seizes wit 
avidity upon objects which allure it, persistently returnin 
after we have withdrawn it from them. If, therefore, instea 
of controlling this restless faculty, we treat it like a spoile 
child, indulging all its caprices, we strengthen its evil ter 
dencies, and in time of prayer we shall vainly seek t 
restrain it. Unaccustomed to pious objects, it will reb 
against us. 

Knowing the dangerous propensities of this power, w 
should vigilantly guard it and cut off from it all unprofitab. 
reflections. To do this effectually we must carefully e: 
amine the thoughts presented to our minds, that we may sé 
which we shall admit and which we shall reject. If we a 
careless in this respect, ideas and sentiments will penetra 
our hearts and not only weaken devotion and diminish fe 
vor, but destroy charity, which is the life of the soul. 

We read in Holy Scripture that while his doorkeepe 
who should have been cleansing wheat, fell aslee 
assassins entered the house of Isboseth, son of Saul, ar 
slew him. (Cf. 2 Kg. 4). A like fate will be ours if we perm

in separating the chaff from the grain—that is, goc

desires, the assassins of the soul, in this manner are able

ter and rob us of the life of grace. 

But this vigilance not only serves to preserve the life of 
= soul, but most efficaciously promotes recollection in 
ayer; for as a wandering and uncontrolled imagination is 
bource of much trouble in prayer, so a subdued imagina- 
mn accustomed to pious subjects sweetens our conversa-

SECTION IX 
The Government of the Understanding 


We have now come to the greatest and noblest of the 
2ulties, the understanding, which raises man above all 
sible creatures, and in which he most resembles his Crea- 
-. The beauty of this power depends upon that rare virtue, 
dence, which excels all others. In the spiritual life pru- 
ice is to the soul what the eyes are to the body, what a 
.Ot is to a vessel, what a head is to a commonwealth. For 
is reason the great St. Anthony, in a conference with 
veral holy monks on the excellence of the virtues, gave 
> first place to prudence, which guides and controls all the 
ners. 

Let him, therefore, who desires to practice the other vir- 
2s with profit earnestly endeavor to be guided by pru- 
ce in all things. Not limited to any special duty, it enters 
o the fulfillment of all duties, into the practice of all vir- 
Ss, and preserves order and harmony among them. Hav- 
the foundation of faith and charity, it first belongs to 
dence to direct all our actions to God, who is our last 
. As self-love, according to a holy writer, seeks self in all 
ngs, even the holiest, prudence is ever ready to examine 
at are the motives of our actions, whether we have God 
self as the end of what we do. 

feaenee also guides us in our intercourse with our 
ighbor, that we may afford him edification and not give 


338 The Sinner’s Guide 


him scandal. To this end it teaches us to observe the condi 
tion and character of those about us, that we may mort 
wisely benefit them, patiently bearing with their failing; 
and closing our eyes to infirmities which we cannot cure 
‘‘A wise man,” says Aristotle, “should not expect the sami 
degree of certainty in all things, for some are more suscepti 
ble of proof than others. Nor should he expect the sami 
degree of perfection in all creatures, for some are capabl 
of a perfection which is impossible in others. Whoever 
therefore, would force all lives to the same standard of vir 
tue would do more harm than good.” 

Prudence also teaches us to know ourselves, our inclina 
tions, our failings, and our evil tendencies, that we may no 
presume upon our strength, but recognizing our enemies 
perseveringly combat them. It is this virtue also which ena 
bles us wisely to govern the tongue by the rules which w 
have already given, teaching us when to be silent and whe 
to speak. Prudence likewise guards us against the error o 
opening our minds to all whom we may meet, or of makin 
confidants of others without due reflection. By putting 
just restraint upon our words, it saves us from too freely ex 
pressing our opinion and thereby committing many fault: 

Thus we are kept constantly reminded of the words c 
Solomon: “A fool uttereth all his mind; a wise man defet 
reth and keepeth it till afterwards.” (Prov. 29:11). Prudenc 
also forearms us against dangers, and strengthens us b 
prayer and meditation to meet all the accidents of life. Th 
is the advice of the sacred writer: “Before sickness take 
medicine.” (Ecclus. 18:20). 

Whenever, therefore, you expect to participate in ente: 
tainments, or to transact business with men who are easil 
angered, or to encounter any danger, endeavor to forese 
the perils of the occasion and arm yourself against then 
Prudence guides us in the treatment of our bodies, causir 
us to observe a just medium between excessive rigor ar

moderate indulgence, so that we may neither unduly 
jaken the flesh nor so strengthen it that it will rule the 
rit. 

lt is also the duty of prudence to introduce moderation 
© all our works, even the holiest, and to preserve us from 
jausting the spirit by indiscreet labor. We read in the 
es of St. Francis that the spirit must rule our occupations, 
‘ be ruled by them. Our exterior labors should never 
se us to lose sight of interior duties, nor should devotion 
our neighbor make us forget what we owe to God. If the 
»ostles, who possessed such abundant grace, deemed it 
pedient to renounce the care of temporal things in order 
devote themselves to the great work of preaching and 
her spiritual functions (Cf. Acts 6:2-4), it is presumption 
us to suppose that we have strength and virtue capable of 
idertaking many arduous labors at one time. 

Finally, prudence enlightens us concerning the snares of 
¢ enemy, counseling us, in the words of the Apostles, “to 
| spirits if they be of God,” “for Satan transformeth him- 
if into an angel of light.” (J Jn. 4:1 and 2 Cor. 11:14). 
iere is no temptation more to be feared than one which 
esents itself under the mask of virtue, and there is none 
ee the devil more frequently employs to deceive pious 
uls. Inspired and guided by prudence, we shall recognize 
se snares; we shall be restrained by a salutary fear from 
ing where there is danger, but animated by a holy 
urage to conquer in every struggle; we shall avoid ex- 
mes; we shall endeavor to prevent our neighbor from 
fering scandal, but yet we shall not be daunted by every 
oundless fear; we shall learn to despise the opinions of the 
orld, and not to fear its outcries against virtue, remember- 
a3 with the Apostle, that if we please men we cannot be 
> servants of Jesus Christ. (Cf. Gal. 1:10).

SECTION X 
Prudence in Temporal Affairs 


The virtue of prudence is no less efficacious in the direc 
tion of temporal affairs. It preserves us from serious, an 
sometimes from irremediable, errors which not unfre 
quently destroy both our material and spiritual welfare. T 
escape this double misfortune, here are the counsels whic. 
prudence suggests: The first is that of the Wise Man, wh 
says: “Let thy eyes look straight on, and let thy eyelids g 
before thy steps.” (Prov. 4:25). In other words, look at th 
enterprise you are about to undertake, and do not rashl 
enter upon it. First recommend it to God; then weigh all it 
circumstances, and the consequences which are likely t 
follow from it; seek counsel of just minds concerning i 
deliberate upon the advice you receive, and reflect upo 
your resolution before acting upon it. 

In a word, beware of the four great enemies of prudence 
precipitation, passion, obstinate persistence in our ow 
opinions, and vanity. Precipitation admits no reasoning 
passion blinds us; obstinancy turns a deaf ear to all counse 
and vanity ruins everything. 

It also belongs to prudence to observe a just medium i 
all things, for extremes are no less opposed to virtue thant 
truth. Let not the faults of a few lead you to condemn th 
multitude, nor should the virtues of a few lead you to suf 
pose that all are pious. Follow the guidance of reason in a 
things, and do not allow yourself to be hurried to extreme 
by passion or prejudice. This latter failing is apt, moreove 
to dispose us favorably towards what is old, and give us 
dislike for what is new. Prudence guards us against this, fe 
age can no more justify what is bad than novelty can cor 
demn what is good. Let us esteem things not for their ag: 
but for their merit. A vice of long standing is only mot 
difficult to eradicate, and a virtue of recent growth has on 


Man’s Duty to Himself 341 


be fault of being unknown. 
Beware also of appearances. There are few who have not 
2en taught by experience how deceptive these often are. 
Finally, let us be thoroughly convinced that as reflection 
id gravity are the inseparable companions of prudence, so 
sShness and levity ever accompany folly. Therefore, we 
‘ust guard against these two faults at all times, but par- 
cularly in the following cases: in believing everything that 
reported, for this indicates levity of mind; in making 
omises, in which we often bind ourselves beyond our 
leans; in giving, in which liberality often makes us forget 
stice; in forming resolutions which from want of con- 
deration often lead us into errors; in conversation, in 
‘hich so many faults may be committed; and in temptations 
» anger, which shows the folly of man. “He that is patient,” 
xys Solomon, “is governed with much wisdom, but he that 
impatient exalteth his folly.” (Prov. 14:29). 
. SECTION XI 
| Means of Acquiring this Virtue 
Not the least important means of acquiring this virtue is 
ue experience of our own failures and the success of others, 
om which we may gather wise lessons of prudence. For 
is reason the past is said to be a wise counselor, for today 
rarns from yesterday. “What is it that hath been? The same 
ing that shall be. What is it that hath been done? The 
me that shall be done.” (Eccles. 1:9). But a still more 
fficacious means of becoming prudent is humility, for 
ride is the greatest obstacle to this virtue. “Where pride is, 
here also shall be reproach,” the Holy Ghost tells us; “but 
here humility is, there also is wisdom.” (Prov. 11:2). And 
nroughout the Scriptures we are frequently reminded that 
iod instructs the humble and reveals His secrets to the 
wly. 


342 The Sinner’s Guide 


Humility, however, does not require us to yield blindly to 
all opinions or indiscreetly to follow every counsel. This is 
not humility, but weakness and instability, against which 
the author of Ecclesiasticus warns us: ‘Be not lowly in thy 
wisdom, lest being humbled thou be deceived into folly.” 
(Ecclus. 13:11). By this we should understand that a man 
must resolutely maintain the truth and vigorously support 
justice, not allowing himself to be carried away by contrary 
opinions. 

Finally, devout and humble prayer will afford us power- 
ful aid in acquiring the virtue of prudence. For the prin- 
cipal office of the Holy Ghost being to enlighten the under- 
standing with the gifts of knowledge, wisdom, and counsel, 
the greater the humility and devotion with which we present 
ourselves before this Divine Spirit, the greater will be the 
grace we shall receive.

Behold how highly the great Apostle extols the virtue of

enumerates its advantages. He gives it the first place among

It would be difficult to say more in praise of charity. Cer:

Charity was also a favorite virtue with the beloved disci.

“He that hath the substance of this world,” says St. John

Among the works comprised in charity to our neighbo 
the following are the most important: advice, counsel, suc 
cor, forbearance, pardon, edification. These are so strongh 
linked with charity that the practice of them indicates thi

There are Christians who pretend to love their neighbor 
but their charity goes no further than words. Others ar 


Man’s Duty to his Neighbor 345 


ifling to give advice, but no more substantial proof of their

It may be said that he who simply loves his neighbor

ounsel possesses the second; he who assists him in poverty 
ir distress possesses the third; he who patiently bears an in- 
ury possesses the fourth; he who freely pardons it, the fifth; 
md he who in addition to all these fulfills the duty of 
idification to his neighbor has attained the highest degree

_ The works of which we have just been treating are what 
re called positive acts of charity, which teach us what we 
ught to do for our neighbor. Besides these there are others, 
alled negative duties, which indicate what we must avoid 
n our intercourse with our neighbor. Such are judging 
shly, speaking evil, using abusive or insulting language, 
hjuring his honor or reputation, and giving scandal by 
vords or evil counsel. If you would fulfill the law of charity, 
void all these. 
To reduce to practice what we have said, let your love for 
our neighbor be like that of a mother for her child. See 
vith what devotion a good mother cares for her child; how 
udently she counsels him in danger; how faithfully she 
ssists him in his necessities; how ingenious she is in regard 


346 The Sinner’s Guide 


to his faults, sometimes patiently bearing them, at other 
times justly punishing them, or again prudently ignoring 
them. How earnestly she rejoices in his prosperity; how 
deeply she grieves at his misfortune as if it were her own! 
How zealous she is for his honor and advancement; how 
fervently she prays for him; how cheerfully she denies her- 
self to give to him; how utterly she forgets herself in her 
care of him! Your charity would be perfect did it resemble 
this. Though you may not attain this degree, you must 
nevertheless aspire to it, for the higher you aim the more 
noble will be your conduct. 

You will doubtless urge that you cannot feel such affec- 
tion for one who is a stranger to you. But you should not 
regard your neighbor as a stranger. Behold in him rather 
the image of God, the work of His divine hands, and a liv- 
ing member of Christ. (Cf. Rom. 12:5). Hence St. Paul tells 
us that when we sin against our neighbor we sin against 
Christ. (Cf. 7 Cor. 8:12). Look on your neighbor, therefore, 
not as a man but as Christ Himself, or one of His living 
members; for though he is not so in body, he is truly so by 
participation in the spirit of Christ, and by the reward 
which is promised to us, for Christ assures us that He will 
consider as done to Himself all that we do to our neighbor. 

Think of the affection which ties of blood establish be- 
tween creatures, and blush to let nature influence you more 
powerfully than grace. You will doubtless urge that your 
relatives are descended with you from the same ancestor, 
and that the same blood flows in your veins. Remember, 
however, that there are closer and stronger bonds uniting us 
as brethren in Christ. In God we have one Father; in the 
Church one mother; and in Jesus Christ one Lord and 
Saviour. One faith springs from the same source which 
enlightens all Christians and distinguishes them from the 
rest of men. 


The object of our hope is the same kingdom, where we

ail have but one heart and one soul. Baptism has made us

CHAPTER 42 
Man’s Duty to God 


SECTION I 
Man’s Duties in General 


The third and noblest obligation of justice comprises 
man’s duty to God, which includes the practice of the three 
theological virtues, faith, hope, and charity, and of that vir- 
tue called religion, which has for its object the worship due 
to God. 

To love God with the affection of a dutiful son is the most 
secure way of fulfilling this obligation, as the most effective 
means of discharging the other duties of justice is to be te 
ourselves an upright judge, and to our neighbor a kind anc 
watchful mother. 

Consider, then, how a good son manifests his love for hi 
father. How great is his devotion, his fear, his reverence fo: 
him! How faithfully he obeys him; how zealously and disin 
terestedly he serves him! With what confidence he goes t 
him in all his necessities! With what submission he accept 
his corrections! How patiently he bears his reproofs! Onhk 
serve God with such a heart, and you will faithfully fulfil 
this obligation of justice. 

But to attain these dispositions the following virtues seen 
to me indispensable: love, holy fear, confidence, zeal fo 
the glory of God, purity of intention, the spirit of prayet 
gratitude, conformity to the will of God, humility, and pa 
tience in tribulation.

The Love of God 


Our first duty is to love God, as He has commanded us, 
th our whole heart, with our whole soul, and with our 
yole strength. (Cf. Deut. 6:5). All our faculties must 
operate in loving and serving this great Master: the 
.derstanding by frequently thinking of Him; the will by 
ying Him; the passions by turning their strength to His 
ice; the senses and members by zealously executing 
natever His love prescribes.

SECTION III 
The Fear of God 


After love comes fear, which in fact springs from love. 
pr the greater our love for another, the greater is our fear 
it only of losing him but of offending him. See how 
irefully a good son avoids anything that could displease 
5 father, or a loving wife all that could displease her hus- 
nd. This fear is the guardian of innocence, and for this 
son we should deeply engrave it in our souls, praying 
th David that the Lord may pierce our flesh with His holy 
r. (Cf. Ps. 118:120). This pious monarch desired that 
en his flesh should be penetrated with this salutary fear, 
t, piercing his heart like a thorn, it might unceasingly 
rn him against all that could lead him to offend God, the 
‘ject of his love and fear. It was for this reason that the in- 
ired author wrote, ‘“‘The fear of the Lord driveth out sin.” 
‘cclus. ao) 

The effect of this fear is not only to make us avoid actions 
at are positively sinful, but even those that may lead us 


350 The Sinner’s Guide 


into evil or endanger our virtue. These words of Job, “I 
feared all my works, knowing that thou didst not spare the 
offender” (Job 9:28), testify how deeply this sentiment was 
imprinted in his soul. 

If we are penetrated with this salutary fear it will be 
manifest in our bearing when we enter God’s house, and 
particularly in the presence of the Blessed Sacrament. We 
shall beware of irreverently talking or gazing about us as if 
we were unconscious of the dread Majesty in whose temple 
we are. 

The love of God, as we have already said, is the first 
source of this fear. Servile fear, however, which is the fear. 
not of a son, but of a slave, is, in a measure, profitable, for it 
introduces filial fear as the needle introduces the thread 
But we shall strengthen and confirm this sentiment of holy 
fear by reflecting upon the incomprehensible majesty of 
God, the severity of His judgments, the rigor of His justice 
the multitude of our sins, and particularly our resistance te 
divine inspirations.

To fear we must also join confidence. Like a child wh«

ourselves into the arms of our Heavenly Father, confiden 
that those Hands which sustain the heavens are all powerfu 
to supply our necessities, to uphold us in temptation, and t 
turn all things to our profit. And why should we not hav 
confidence in God? Is He not the most powerful as well a 
the most tender of fathers? If your want of merit and th

thoughts upon the goodness of God, upon His adorabl 
Son, our Redeemer and Mediator, who died to expiate ou 
sins.

When you are crossing a rapid stream, and the turbulence
